	<item>
		<title>Managed NVIDIA Dynamo on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/managed-nvidia-dynamo-on-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vinay Arora]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 23:33:26 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">7d273ceec71d93e4d51e244ed3e0dd70fd6b8ef7</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference now supports NVIDIA Dynamo for distributed inference, with multi-node and disaggregated serving, KV-aware routing, and automatic fault recovery. Serve large models across many nodes without building the orchestration layer yourself.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Serving large language models (LLMs) efficiently requires sufficient GPU memory and careful coordination of prompt processing and token generation. GLM-5.3’s FP8 weights, for example, exceed the 640 GB of GPU memory on a single &lt;code&gt;ml.p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance, before accounting for KV cache and runtime overhead. Long prompts can also slow token generation when both share the same GPUs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference now supports &lt;a href="https://github.com/ai-dynamo/dynamo" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA Dynamo&lt;/a&gt;, combining Dynamo’s distributed inference capabilities with the infrastructure management of HyperPod to address these challenges. Dynamo is an open source distributed inference framework that supports multi-node deployment, disaggregated prefill and decode, and KV-aware routing with engines such as vLLM, SGLang, and TensorRT-LLM. HyperPod supports these deployments with high-performance Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) networking, coordinated GPU scheduling, and node health monitoring and recovery. In this post, we illustrate multi-node and disaggregated serving through a deployment example, share benchmark results, and outline best practices for optimizing inference on HyperPod.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-the-integration-works"&gt;How the integration works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The HyperPod Inference add-on installs the Dynamo custom resource definitions and these serving and scheduling components:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;HyperPod Inference Operator &lt;/em&gt;manages endpoint deployment through an InferenceEndpointConfig (IEC) custom resource. Its &lt;code&gt;dynamoSpec&lt;/code&gt; section supplies the Dynamo configuration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;Dynamo operator &lt;/em&gt;manages resources defined in a &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/dynamo/dev/kubernetes/model-deployment/deploy-with-dgd" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DynamoGraphDeployment (DGD)&lt;/a&gt;, including the frontend, model workers, replica counts, and resource requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/ai-dynamo/grove" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Grove&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/em&gt;coordinates related groups of pods, including multi-node workers, and their scaling relationships.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/kai-scheduler/KAI-Scheduler" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kubernetes AI (KAI) Scheduler&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/em&gt;places pods on GPU resources, using gang scheduling to schedule the pods required for a multi-node worker together.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers (DLCs) provide the Dynamo runtime, while the add-on manages integrated component versions. To adopt a supported Dynamo release, update the add-on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deployment-architecture"&gt;Deployment architecture&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the deployment paths, serving components, and infrastructure management for disaggregated inference on SageMaker HyperPod. The Dynamo frontend routes requests to prefill workers, which process prompts, and decode workers, which generate tokens. These separate pools allow independent scaling and tuning. Both pools load the full model weights, and KV cache transfers between them over EFA.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dynamo also supports aggregated serving, where prefill and decode run in the same worker. In either topology, workers can run on one instance or span multiple instances using tensor and pipeline parallelism. Dynamo’s &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/dynamo/dev/knowledge-base/concepts/system-architecture/kv-aware-routing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KV-aware router&lt;/a&gt; considers reusable prefix KV cache and active worker load in both topologies. Prefix reuse can reduce prompt recomputation and improve time to first token, while load awareness helps avoid overloading workers. For more details on the Dynamo architecture, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/dynamo/knowledge-base/overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dynamo Knowledge Base overview&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;HyperPod provides node, GPU, and EFA health monitoring and manages node replacement, while the orchestration components recreate affected workers. Deployments configured through InferenceEndpointConfig with &lt;code&gt;dynamoSpec&lt;/code&gt; support invocation through SageMaker endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 748px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/14/ML-22230-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/14/ML-22230-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of NVIDIA Dynamo on SageMaker HyperPod with the frontend routing to prefill and decode workers over EFA" width="738"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Deployment paths, serving components, and infrastructure management for NVIDIA Dynamo on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="two-ways-to-configure-your-deployment"&gt;Two ways to configure your deployment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Whether you are new to HyperPod, new to Dynamo, or both, the add-on offers two deployment paths to fit your workflow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Bring-your-own DynamoGraphDeployment (DGD)&lt;/em&gt; lets existing Dynamo users continue using upstream documentation, recipes, and manifests, with the Dynamo operator managing the deployment. To bring an existing DGD to HyperPod, use a supported container image and configure storage, networking, and pod placement for your cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig with dynamoSpec&lt;/em&gt; lets existing HyperPod Inference users deploy a new Dynamo endpoint using the same configuration format and OpenAI-compatible interface they already know. For the inference engine, you can choose vLLM, SGLang, or TensorRT-LLM, with vLLM used by default. The operator also supplies defaults for KV-aware routing and replica counts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both paths support single-instance and multi-node deployments. In either case, HyperPod manages node health and replacement. The table summarizes the responsibilities for each path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bring-your-own DGD&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig with dynamoSpec&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;You write&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Complete DGD manifest&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;IEC containing dynamoSpec&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Best for&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Teams wanting direct control of the serving graph or reuse of existing DGDs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Teams wanting managed endpoint setup through IEC&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model weights&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;You configure downloading or mounting the weights&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The operator stages weights from Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) or Amazon FSx&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Endpoint, TLS, registration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;You configure and manage these resources&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Managed through the HyperPod Inference Operator&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Autoscaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;You configure and manage scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Kubernetes Event-driven Autoscaling (KEDA) through &lt;code&gt;autoScalingSpec&lt;/code&gt; for aggregated and disaggregated deployments&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deployment-example-glm-5.3"&gt;Deployment example: GLM-5.3&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following excerpts use the InferenceEndpointConfig (IEC) deployment path with vLLM on &lt;code&gt;ml.p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances. Dynamo-specific settings belong under &lt;code&gt;spec.dynamoSpec&lt;/code&gt;. Model source and endpoint settings are included in the complete manifest.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multi-node-aggregated-serving"&gt;Multi-node aggregated serving&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This configuration runs one model-worker replica across two instances (16 GPUs), with two frontend replicas for request handling. Setting &lt;code&gt;dynamoSpec.frontend.routingMode&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;kv-aware&lt;/code&gt; turns on KV-aware routing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;spec:
  replicas: 1
  dynamoSpec:
    modelServer: vllm
    frontend:
      replicas: 2
      routingMode: kv-aware
    aggregation:
      nodesPerReplica: 2
      tensorParallelism: 16&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here, &lt;code&gt;spec.replicas&lt;/code&gt; controls the number of model-worker replicas, while &lt;code&gt;frontend.replicas&lt;/code&gt; controls the number of frontend replicas.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multi-node-disaggregated-serving"&gt;Multi-node disaggregated serving&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;aggregation&lt;/code&gt; under &lt;code&gt;spec.dynamoSpec&lt;/code&gt; with the following &lt;code&gt;disaggregation&lt;/code&gt; block, keeping the preceding frontend configuration. Replica counts are set separately for prefill and decode. This example runs one prefill replica and two decode replicas, each spanning two instances, for a total of six instances.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;disaggregation:
  prefill:
    nodesPerReplica: 2
    tensorParallelism: 16
    replicas: 1
  decode:
    nodesPerReplica: 2
    tensorParallelism: 16
    replicas: 2&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="apply-the-deployment"&gt;Apply the deployment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Apply the complete manifest with &lt;code&gt;kubectl apply&lt;/code&gt;. The HyperPod Inference Operator makes the configured model weights available to the workers and generates the DGD. Grove and KAI Scheduler coordinate placement so that the pods required for each multi-node worker are scheduled together.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the SageMaker endpoint is ready and configured for chat completions, clients can send an OpenAI-compatible request payload through the SageMaker Runtime API:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker-runtime invoke-endpoint \
--endpoint-name glm53 \
--content-type application/json \
--body '{"model":"zai-org/GLM-5.3","messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Hello"}],"max_tokens":128}' \
--cli-binary-format raw-in-base64-out \
out.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="benchmarks"&gt;Benchmarks&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We benchmarked Dynamo on SageMaker HyperPod across two models to compare serving configurations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="disaggregated-prefill-and-decode-for-qwen3-235b"&gt;Disaggregated prefill and decode for Qwen3-235B&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We used &lt;a href="https://github.com/ai-dynamo/aiperf" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AIPerf&lt;/a&gt; to compare aggregated and disaggregated serving for &lt;code&gt;Qwen3-235B-A22B-FP8&lt;/code&gt; using Dynamo with TensorRT-LLM on two p5en.48xlarge instances (16 H200 GPUs). Requests contained 4,000 input tokens and 200 output tokens. We retained measured points that met two targets: P90 time to first token below one second, and median generation speed of at least 50 tokens per second per user. Among these points, disaggregated serving achieved 2.05× the highest aggregated throughput: 207 versus 101 output tokens per second per GPU, at concurrency 80 and 32, respectively.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/14/ML-22230-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/14/ML-22230-2.png" alt="Throughput comparison for Qwen3-235B: disaggregated serving at 207 versus aggregated at 101 output tokens per second per GPU" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Aggregated compared to disaggregated serving throughput for Qwen3-235B-A22B-FP8&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multi-node-aggregated-serving-for-qwen3.5-397b"&gt;Multi-node aggregated serving for Qwen3.5-397B&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dynamo supports multi-node serving in both aggregated and disaggregated modes for models that exceed a single instance’s GPU memory. To evaluate a worker spanning nodes, we used AIPerf to benchmark multi-node aggregated serving for Qwen3.5-397B-A17B-FP8 with Dynamo and vLLM, using TP4 × PP2 and eight active H200 GPUs. This model fits on one instance, so in practice you would not split it. It serves here as a controlled baseline. We compared eight GPUs on one p5en.48xlarge against four GPUs on each of two instances, using the same model, parallelism, and workload configuration. With an agentic workload featuring high prefix reuse at concurrency 32, the two-node configuration delivered 126.4 output tokens per second per GPU, retaining 99.1 percent of single-node throughput. This shows that Dynamo on SageMaker HyperPod can serve a model that needs more than one instance across nodes. The node boundary itself does not become a significant throughput penalty.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/14/ML-22230-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/14/ML-22230-3.png" alt="Throughput comparison for Qwen3.5-397B: two-node serving at 126.4 output tokens per second per GPU, 99.1% of single-node throughput" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Single-node compared to two-node throughput for multi-node aggregated serving of Qwen3.5-397B-A17B-FP8&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices-for-optimized-inference"&gt;Best practices for optimized inference&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA Dynamo provides recipes and tools to help you select a serving configuration, benchmark performance, and tune your deployment on SageMaker HyperPod.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Start with a matching recipe –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Choose a &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/dynamo/dev/recipes/browse" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dynamo recipe&lt;/a&gt; for your model, inference engine, and GPU type. Account for model weights, KV cache, and runtime memory when sizing workers. For supported configurations, &lt;a href="https://github.com/ai-dynamo/aisimulate" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AISimulate&lt;/a&gt; can help compare aggregated and disaggregated serving, choose parallelism, and size prefill and decode worker pools before testing on GPUs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Verify communication before benchmarking –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;For deployments spanning instances, confirm that communication uses the intended EFA transport, including KV-cache transfers between prefill and decode workers. See the &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/dynamo/dev/kubernetes/installation/rdma-setup/efa-on-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dynamo EFA guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Benchmark representative traffic –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Replay your traffic with &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/aiperf/welcome-to-ai-perf-documentation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AIPerf&lt;/a&gt;, or use a &lt;a href="https://github.com/ai-dynamo/aiperf/blob/main/docs/tutorials/agentic-code-generator.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;generated agentic trace&lt;/a&gt; with representative prompt lengths, output lengths, and prefix reuse. Sweep concurrency for each configuration, retain points meeting your SLAs, and choose based on cost and latency priorities.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tune from measured bottlenecks –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Adjust topology and worker allocation before engine settings, changing one variable at a time. Follow the &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/dynamo/dev/kubernetes/operations/performance-tuning" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;performance tuning guide&lt;/a&gt;, or use &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/dynamo/dev/agent-skills/optimization" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dynamo optimization agent skills&lt;/a&gt; to assist with benchmarking and tuning. Validate each candidate on your target HyperPod deployment.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA Dynamo on SageMaker HyperPod Inference simplifies deploying and operating large models with multi-node serving, disaggregated prefill and decode, and KV-aware routing. The managed add-on installs the serving components, and configuration defaults simplify endpoint setup. HyperPod manages model weights, auto-scales clusters based on GPU and inference metrics, monitors node health, and replaces failed nodes. Together, these capabilities reduce undifferentiated infrastructure work so teams can focus on their models and applications. Get started with the setup instructions and complete deployment examples in the linked repository.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Reducing token costs and latency in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore JSON workloads</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reducing-token-costs-and-latency-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-json-workloads/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Pallav Gupta]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 22:44:51 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">cf1bf852a941654f24f9ad7847ac33595e998883</guid>

					<description>As production agents on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore process multi-megabyte JSON payloads, token costs and latency climb. This post shares three composable strategies: offloading heavy artifacts to Amazon S3, on-demand result paging, and dictionary compaction. Together they cut per-turn token use by up to 71% and latency by up to 80%.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;As enterprises build production agents with dozens of tools and large structured inputs, token consumption and latency become the primary blockers to a good user experience. When these agents process large JSON data, every additional tool invocation injects the full payload into the large language model (LLM) context window, causing token usage and response latency to spike. Over multi-turn conversations, this pressure compounds, driving up costs and degrading response quality. Effectively managing context-window pressure in agentic systems is key to reliability and responsiveness at scale. In this post, we share three composable strategies we used to build a production agent for Flightscape that handles 20 MB+ JSON payloads. You can apply them to reduce token costs, lower latency, and maintain full reasoning capability across extended sessions. Together, these optimizations reduced token consumption by up to 71% and cut average latency by up to 80% across our benchmark runs on a representative 23 MB payload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Flightscape is an airline operations platform that helps airlines plan and manage their crews, flights, and operations. As part of Flightscape’s crew management capabilities, we built a Training Schedule Optimization agent that verifies crew training schedule assignments against business rules. With 40+ tools and JSON inputs in megabytes, this agent could answer complex queries across crew assignments, scheduling, and resource allocation. Although the agent delivered the required functionality, average request latencies exceeded 15 seconds across most calls. Every tool call injected its full payload into the model context, inflating token counts and costs. On larger datasets, the context window filled up before the agent could finish reasoning. The following diagram (Figure 1) details the agentic architecture and data flow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram: the optimization workflow outputs request, response, and validation report JSON, which an AI agent reads to answer user questions through a chat interface" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Agent architecture and data flow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In Figure 1, the Optimizer receives a Request JSON and produces a Response JSON, which is then validated by a Business Rules Validator to generate a Validation Report JSON. All three JSON documents are ingested by an AI Agent that provides a conversational interface for users to query optimization results, understand validation errors, and explore scheduling assignments. The agent is built with &lt;a href="https://www.langchain.com/langgraph" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangGraph&lt;/a&gt;, an open source framework by LangChain, and runs on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. Amazon &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; can be applied to validate and filter the agent’s conversational responses, helping facilitate responsible AI practices in production deployments. We defined a custom agent state that holds the data needed by our tools: optimization request JSON, optimization response JSON, and validation report JSON. We also added basic statistics and cache data to state for rich queries. The agent uses an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; model for reasoning and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/memory.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore memory&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, to persist chat interactions across turns, and each turn follows this flow (Figure 2):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-21786-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-21786-2.png" alt="Diagram of one agent turn: hydrate session state, run tool calls, then dehydrate state back to storage" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Agentic flow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is what the state structure looks like. The state references TOPT (Training Optimizer) request and response objects, which represent the crew scheduling inputs and optimization results:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;class GraphState(TypedDict):
    """LangGraph state that combines messages with application data."""
    # Messages from conversation (managed by add_messages reducer)
    messages: Annotated[List[BaseMessage], add_messages]
    # Application-specific state - parsed domain objects
    request: Optional[TOPTRequest]
    responses: List[TOPTResponse]
    validation_report: Optional[Dict[str, Any]]
    basic_stats: Optional[Dict[str, Any]]
    cache_data: Optional[Dict[str, Any]]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; This state originally held heavy payloads inline. Later sections explain how we moved large artifacts out of conversational context to reduce token usage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We implemented three strategies to reduce the token costs, latency, and memory overhead caused by passing large JSON payloads through the model context. The following diagram (Figure 3) shows how the three strategies fit into a single agent turn. Strategy 1 runs at the start and end of each turn, and Strategies 2 and 3 run each time a tool returns a result.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-21786-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-21786-3.png" alt="Flow diagram of one agent turn. At the start of the turn, hydrate_state() restores lightweight session state from AgentCore memory. Tool results are checked for size: oversized lists return a count, a sample, and a handle (Strategy 2), and oversized dictionaries return a compact descriptor (Strategy 3). The model can fetch more data with get_result_page_tool(). At the end of the turn, dehydrate_state() saves references to AgentCore memory and heavy payloads to the S3ArtifactStore in Amazon S3." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Overview of the three optimization strategies in one agent turn&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the start of a turn, the &lt;code&gt;hydrate_state()&lt;/code&gt; function restores lightweight session state from AgentCore memory. When a tool returns an oversized list or dictionary, the agent stores the full result in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt; and passes the model a compact summary with a handle. The model can then retrieve more detail with &lt;code&gt;get_result_page_tool()&lt;/code&gt;. At the end of the turn, the &lt;code&gt;dehydrate_state()&lt;/code&gt; function writes heavy payloads to the &lt;code&gt;S3ArtifactStore&lt;/code&gt; and saves only references to AgentCore memory. The following sections describe each strategy in detail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To implement these strategies, make sure the following are in place:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to Amazon Bedrock and a supported model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon S3 bucket for artifact storage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore memory enabled for session-state persistence.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A LangGraph-based agent (or equivalent graph-orchestration framework) with tool-calling support.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Python 3.12+ environment with &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;langchain-core&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;langgraph&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="optimization-strategies"&gt;Optimization strategies&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The companion code is available in this &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-reducing-token-costs-and-latency-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-json-workloads" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="strategy-1-persist-lightweight-session-state-offload-heavy-artifacts"&gt;Strategy 1: Persist lightweight session state, offload heavy artifacts&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our first optimization was to change how we persist session state. Initially, large payloads were being stored too close to the conversational state. We therefore moved heavy artifacts (request JSON, response JSON, validation data, and other large derived payloads) to Amazon S3. AgentCore memory then persists only stable references to these objects, keeping the session state lightweight.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To support this, we introduced a dedicated persisted-state schema that keeps only lightweight fields and artifact references. The live graph state still includes parsed domain objects for tool execution, but persistence now writes a dehydrated form. At runtime, tools hydrate heavy payloads on demand using those references, instead of loading everything on every turn.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This &lt;code&gt;PersistedState&lt;/code&gt; schema is what gets written to AgentCore Memory:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;class PersistedState(TypedDict, total=False):
    """Serialized session state for AgentCore Memory.

    Heavy fields (request, responses) are stored in S3 and referenced by key.
    Lightweight fields are stored inline.
    """
    messages: List[Any]  # LangChain-serialized messages
    request_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key for raw request JSON
    responses_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key for raw responses JSON list
    cache_data_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key for cache_data payload
    validation_report_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key for validation_report payload
    basic_stats_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key for basic_stats payload&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The updated &lt;code&gt;GraphState&lt;/code&gt; mirrors these references alongside the live domain objects:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;class GraphState(TypedDict):
    """LangGraph state that combines messages with application data."""
    messages: Annotated[List[BaseMessage], add_messages]
    # ... (same domain fields as original graph state)
    # Artifact references for persistence
    request_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key reference for raw request JSON
    responses_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key reference for raw responses JSON list
    cache_data_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key ref for cache_data payload
    validation_report_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key ref for validation_report payload
    basic_stats_ref: Optional[str]  # S3 key ref for basic_stats payload&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;dehydrate_state&lt;/code&gt; function strips heavy payloads from the live state and returns the lightweight persisted form along with the artifacts to upload:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def dehydrate_state(state: GraphState) -&amp;gt; Tuple[PersistedState, Dict[str, Any]]:
    persisted: PersistedState = {
        "messages": [dumpd(m) for m in state.get("messages") or []],
        "request_ref": state.get("request_ref"),
        "responses_ref": state.get("responses_ref"),
        # ... (remaining ref fields)
    }
    artifacts: Dict[str, Any] = {}
    if state.get("request_raw") is not None:
        artifacts["request_ref"] = state["request_raw"]
    # ... (see GitHub repository for full implementation)
    return persisted, artifacts&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Rehydration reverses this process. When a session resumes, we rebuild the live graph state: deserialize LangChain messages, re-parse raw payloads into domain objects, and restore every reference key so later turns can locate their artifacts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def hydrate_state(
    persisted: PersistedState,
    request_raw: Optional[Dict[str, Any]] = None,
    responses_raw: Optional[List[Dict[str, Any]]] = None,
) -&amp;gt; GraphState:
    """Reconstruct a live GraphState from its persisted form."""
    messages = [load(m, allowed_objects="messages") for m in persisted.get("messages") or []]
    request = TOPTRequest.from_dict(request_raw) if request_raw else None
    responses = [TOPTResponse.from_dict(r) for r in (responses_raw or [])]
    return {
        "messages": messages, "request": request, "responses": responses,
        # ... (remaining fields restored from persisted refs, see GitHub repository)
    }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both dehydration and rehydration rely on the artifact store returning a consistent reference for each payload. Instead of minting a random key, we derive the reference from the payload itself. This approach, known as content addressing, serializes the JSON with sorted keys and compact separators, and uses the SHA-256 digest of those bytes as the reference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;_content_hash&lt;/code&gt; helper produces a deterministic SHA-256 reference from a JSON-serializable payload:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def _content_hash(payload: Any) -&amp;gt; str:
    return hashlib.sha256(_canonical_bytes(payload)).hexdigest()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Content addressing makes writes idempotent. Identical payloads map to the same key, so a turn that re-serializes an unchanged request JSON reuses the object already in the bucket instead of uploading it again. The S3-backed store exposes a &lt;code&gt;put_json&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;get_json&lt;/code&gt; interface. It also keeps a small in-process cache, so payloads already read or written during the current invocation do not require a second S3 round trip.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;S3ArtifactStore&lt;/code&gt; class wraps the &lt;code&gt;put_json&lt;/code&gt;/&lt;code&gt;get_json&lt;/code&gt; interface with an in-process cache and content-addressed keys:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;class S3ArtifactStore:
    """Content-addressed artifact store backed by Amazon S3."""
    def __init__(self, bucket=None, prefix=None, region=None) -&amp;gt; None:
        self.bucket = bucket or os.getenv("SESSION_STATE_BUCKET")
        self.prefix = (prefix or os.getenv("ARTIFACT_STORE_PREFIX", "artifacts")).strip("/")
        # ... (boto3 session setup, see GitHub repository)
        self._cache: Dict[str, Any] = {}

    def put_json(self, payload: Any) -&amp;gt; str:
        ref = _content_hash(payload)
        with self._lock:
            if ref in self._cache:
                return ref  # Identical content already uploaded, skip PUT
            self._s3.put_object(Bucket=self.bucket, Key=self._key(ref), Body=_canonical_bytes(payload))
            # ... (cache update)
            return ref

    def get_json(self, ref: str) -&amp;gt; Any:
        with self._lock:
            if ref in self._cache:
                return self._cache[ref]
        # ... (S3 fetch and cache, see GitHub repository)

artifact_store = S3ArtifactStore()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Offloading heavy artifacts to S3 and persisting only lightweight references reduced per-turn latency and the volume of data stored in AgentCore memory. With heavy payloads no longer persisted inline, tool-call responses became the dominant consumer of context tokens, prompting us to introduce result paging.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="strategy-2-page-large-result-lists-on-demand"&gt;Strategy 2: Page large result lists on demand&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Even after moving heavy payload persistence to Amazon S3, we still had a major token bottleneck at tool-response time. Many tool results were too large, which created two problems. First, we were sending full lists to the model even when it only needed a small subset, making each turn slower and more expensive. Second, those full list payloads were injected into context, which quickly consumed the context window over multi-turn conversations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In some cases, a single tool response exceeded 80,000 tokens, and the cost compounded as users continued interacting with the agent. We wanted to preserve full data access without sending everything by default. To solve this, we introduced on-demand result paging for large tool outputs. Instead of returning full lists, tools now return a compact payload with total count, a small sample, and a result handle.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a tool result exceeds the sample-size threshold, the full list is stored in S3 and only a compact summary is returned to the model:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;if len(result) &amp;gt; _LLM_SAMPLE_SIZE:
    handle = artifact_store.put_json(result)
    return json.dumps(
        {
            "_oversized": True,
            "_result_handle": handle,
            "total": len(result),
            "sample": result[:_LLM_SAMPLE_SIZE],
        },
        default=str,
    )&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The handle points to the complete result stored in S3. When the agent needs more detail, it calls a paging tool with the handle and requests only the required slice, for example using offset and limit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;get_result_page_tool&lt;/code&gt; function lets the model retrieve additional slices of a stored result by handle, offset, and limit:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def get_result_page_tool(handle: str, field: Optional[str] = None, offset: int = 0, limit: int = 50):
    payload = artifact_store.get_json(handle)
    if isinstance(payload, list):
        return {
            "handle": handle, "offset": offset, "limit": limit,
            "total": len(payload),
            "items": payload[offset: offset + limit],
        }
    # ... (dict field extraction, see GitHub repository)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On-demand paging kept the full list in S3 and served only the requested slice per turn, controlling context growth and significantly reducing token usage. However, not all oversized outputs were lists. Some tools returned large dictionaries that required a different compaction approach.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="strategy-3-compact-oversized-dictionary-outputs"&gt;Strategy 3: Compact oversized dictionary outputs&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Unlike lists, dictionary outputs contain deeply nested structured data that are useful for reasoning but typically too expensive to include verbatim on each turn. We needed a compact representation that preserved structural cues while drastically cutting the token footprint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To solve this, we introduced descriptor-based compaction for oversized dictionary outputs. Instead of returning an oversized dictionary in full, we return a compact descriptor:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;List fields are represented by count and sample.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Nested dictionaries are summarized by key count.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Scalar values remain inline where useful.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full payload is stored in the artifact store, and the response includes a result handle. When the agent needs deeper detail, it uses that handle to fetch only the required data. This response format remained navigable for the model while significantly reducing unnecessary token usage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;_oversized_descriptor&lt;/code&gt; function builds a compact structural summary of a dictionary payload, preserving field types and sample data without the full content:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def _oversized_descriptor(payload: Dict[str, Any], handle: str) -&amp;gt; Dict[str, Any]:
    fields: Dict[str, Any] = {}
    for key, value in payload.items():
        if key.startswith("_"):
            continue
        if isinstance(value, list):
            fields[key] = {"type": "list", "count": len(value), "sample": value[:5]}
        elif isinstance(value, dict):
            fields[key] = {"type": "dict", "keys": len(value)}
        else:
            fields[key] = {"type": "scalar", "value": value}
    return {"_oversized": True, "_result_handle": handle, "fields": fields}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the serialized output still exceeds the character limit, the fallback stores the full payload and replaces the response with its descriptor:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;content = json.dumps(llm_view, default=str)
if len(content) &amp;gt; _MAX_TOOL_CONTENT_CHARS:
    if handle is None:
        handle = artifact_store.put_json(full_view)
    content = json.dumps(_oversized_descriptor(full_view, handle), default=str)
return content&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With lightweight persisted state, on-demand paging, and dictionary compaction in place, token growth became predictable and the agent stayed responsive even on multi-megabyte datasets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To validate the impact of these three strategies, we ran the same set of questions over the same dataset against both the pre-optimization and optimized versions of the agent. We then compared token usage and latency in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability (a capability of AgentCore), and observed clear improvements in both cost and responsiveness. &lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Across a 5-question conversation run, total token usage dropped by approximately 71%, from ~289,500 to ~82,900 tokens, while average trace latency decreased by nearly 80%, from 15.4 s to 3.1 s (Figure 4). Across 100 sample questions (Figure 5), total tokens fell from 23.6 M to 8.5 M (64%) and average trace latency dropped from 7,930 ms to 2,461 ms (69%).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-4.png" alt="Bar chart comparing total token usage and average latency before and after optimization for a 5-question run" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: OTEL sessions comparison&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-5.png" alt="Bar chart comparing total tokens and average latency before and after optimization across 100 sample questions" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: OTEL sessions comparison across 100 sample questions&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Before&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Input tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~32,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~8,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;75%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Output tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~2,800&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;477&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;83%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Turn latency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~165 s&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 1: Input, output, and latency impact&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 6 and Figure 7 show the corresponding trace details before and after optimization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-6.png" alt="Observability trace timeline before optimization showing many spans and long per-turn latency" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;

 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-7.png" alt="Observability trace timeline after optimization showing fewer spans and much shorter latency" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: After trace detail&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These gains came primarily from avoiding full payload injection into model context at every turn. Instead, the model now receives compact summaries first and fetches additional detail only when needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-8.png" alt="Trace event showing the agent receiving a compact summary with a result handle instead of the full payload" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: Compact summary event&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The improvement was also visible in the trace timelines: the pre-optimization turn stretched to nearly 2 minutes 46 seconds, while the optimized turn completed in about 15 seconds (Figure 9).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-9.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ml-21786-Figure-9.png" alt="Side-by-side trace timelines: about 2 minutes 46 seconds before optimization versus about 15 seconds after" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: Before and after timeline&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The changes also improved behavior in longer conversations. Context growth became more predictable across turns, and the agent remained responsive even when users asked follow-up questions over large datasets. In practice, this shifted the system from compounding token cost to a more controlled, demand-driven access pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most importantly, these gains came without sacrificing capability. Full payloads remain in artifact storage and can be retrieved through handles whenever deeper inspection is needed. At Flightscape, this pattern provides a foundation for bringing conversational AI capabilities to complex airline operations workflows, keeping the underlying AI experience scalable and cost efficient.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources you created for this walkthrough:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Empty and optionally delete the S3 bucket used for the artifact store, or remove all objects under the artifact prefix (for example, artifacts/).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete any Amazon Bedrock AgentCore agents and associated AgentCore memory sessions created during testing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management&lt;/a&gt; (IAM) roles and policies created for S3 or Bedrock access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; log groups generated by the agent runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove any &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Virtual Private Cloud&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon VPC) endpoints or networking resources provisioned for this walkthrough.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your Amazon Bedrock AgentCore agent handles large JSON data, start by identifying which tool outputs dominate your context window. Begin with offloading artifacts to S3 (Strategy 1) because it provides the foundation for the other optimizations, then add paging (Strategy 2) and compaction (Strategy 3) as needed to improve the token usage and latency of your agents. To get started, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-reducing-token-costs-and-latency-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-json-workloads" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; and adapt the strategies to your own agent. To learn more about building agents with AgentCore and LangGraph, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-ai/langgraph" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangGraph framework documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Cut generative AI costs: Code for rules, models for judgment</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/cut-generative-ai-costs-code-for-rules-models-for-judgment/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Meghan Kothari]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 21:14:55 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Strands Agents]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">9aa8427cabe07b8fbd08a7874e7f964a60a8738a</guid>

					<description>The biggest cost lever in a generative AI system is whether you call a model at all. Learn how to decide whether each workflow step needs a model, and map each option to AWS: deterministic code, a constrained decision call, or a right-sized model on Amazon Bedrock.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;The biggest cost lever in a generative AI system is whether you call a model at all. We often see teams route every task through a large language model (LLM), including input validation, formatting, and routing. Each call is cheap in isolation, but at production scale these small costs become one of the largest line items in the architecture. Wherever a deterministic rule can do the job, skipping the model saves money, cuts latency, and removes a source of non-determinism. In this post, you learn how to decide whether each step in a workflow needs a model at all. You can then map each option to AWS: deterministic code, a constrained decision call, or a right-sized model on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-unneeded-model-calls-add-up"&gt;Why unneeded model calls add up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pattern usually starts in a prototype. A model call works for one step, so it becomes the default for the next. Those steps include validating input, reformatting a request body, and picking a route. At prototype volume, nobody notices the cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At production volume, unneeded model calls cost you in three ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Spend grows with request count, even for low-value steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Latency stacks, because every model call in a chain adds hundreds of milliseconds.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Output can vary, because the same input can return a different answer. That makes testing harder and audits slower.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each new model launch then restarts the debate about which model to use. The earlier question, whether the step needs a model at all, goes unasked.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cost guidance, including the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/generative-ai-lens/genperf02-bp03.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Well-Architected Generative AI Lens&lt;/a&gt;, often starts at model selection. This post starts one step earlier: Does the step need a model at all?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-to-decide-whether-a-step-needs-a-model"&gt;How to decide whether a step needs a model&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the whole decision as a flowchart with three labeled lanes: Runs as code, Questions, and Runs on a model. Take each workflow step through three questions in order, and stop at the first option that meets the step’s requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Can a rule check success, such as a format, a schema, a keyword, or a range? If a rule settles only part of the step, a fast path runs the rule first.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Can a model write the rule once, such as a regular expression or a JSON schema?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Does the step pick one answer from a fixed list, such as a route, a class, or yes or no?&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the answer to all three is no, the step is open-ended and runs on a right-sized model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ML-22259-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ML-22259-1.png" alt="Flowchart that sorts a workflow step into code, a constrained decision call, or a right-sized Amazon Bedrock model" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Deciding whether a workflow step needs a model, and which one&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider a customer support assistant. The following table shows how five of its steps go through the chart.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;#&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Path through the chart&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Where it runs&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Check an order number’s format&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A rule can check success.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/api-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon API Gateway&lt;/a&gt; REST API request validation, or an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt; function&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Route a message by intent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A rule settles part of it. A keyword match on the fast path resolves messages such as “refund”, and the residual goes to the third question.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/step-functions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Step Functions&lt;/a&gt; Choice state that uses JSONata. The residual goes to a constrained decision call.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Extract a tracking number from a carrier email&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A model can write the rule once. It drafts a regular expression, and you review and commit it.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Code, as a committed pattern in AWS Lambda&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Pick one of four support queues for a ticket&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The answer comes from a fixed list.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A constrained decision call. Unsure answers escalate to the right-sized model.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Draft a reply to the customer&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The task is open-ended.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The right-sized model, the smallest Amazon Bedrock model that meets the requirements&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can use a model when a task is open-ended, ambiguous, or generative: summarizing a document, drafting a reply, extracting meaning from prose. Use deterministic code when the task has a checkable rule: an email format, a schema, an exact keyword, a numeric range. The following table shows what you trade away when you send a rule-shaped task to a model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Property&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deterministic code&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model call&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost per invocation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Typically orders of magnitude lower&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-token, non-trivial at scale&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Latency&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Single-digit milliseconds or less&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hundreds of milliseconds&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Correctness on a well-specified rule&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Exact for the rule as written&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;High, but with a non-zero error rate&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Auditability&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The rule sits in source control&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Harder to explain to an auditor&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Behavior over time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Stable until you change it&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Changes when you move to a new model version, or when a version is retired&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A model can also hallucinate and return different answers to the same input. A temperature of 0 makes varied answers less likely, but it doesn’t guarantee identical output. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/evaluations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock model evaluation&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; help you measure and contain these risks. A rule has neither problem, which is one reason regulated workloads favor rules. A useful test: If you can write the acceptance test as a rule, you can usually write the step as a rule. For more information, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prescriptive-guidance/latest/agentic-ai-serverless/orchestration-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Orchestration models: From rule-based to AI-native&lt;/a&gt; in AWS Prescriptive Guidance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="decide-dont-generate"&gt;Decide, don’t generate&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most routing and classification steps sit between a rule and open-ended generation. A rule can’t decide every case, but these steps don’t need generated text either. They need one answer from a fixed list of options that you define in advance, like the support queues in the preceding example. Use a component that limits its output to the allowed answers and gives you a signal for when to escalate. Confident answers proceed, and uncertain ones escalate. The AWS Well-Architected Agentic AI Lens describes this &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentcost02-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;tiered, confidence-based escalation&lt;/a&gt;. On AWS, you can build it four ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A small Amazon Bedrock model with structured outputs –&lt;/strong&gt; The answer is limited to an &lt;code&gt;enum&lt;/code&gt; of options, plus an ‘unsure’ option that triggers escalation. This is the model’s own judgment, not a calibrated score, so check it against labeled data before you use it to decide escalation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Embedding similarity –&lt;/strong&gt; Embed the input with an Amazon Bedrock embeddings model and pick the nearest labeled example. Use the similarity score as an escalation signal, and set its threshold on labeled data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-labs/strands-decider" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Decider&lt;/a&gt;, an experimental open source decision model under the Apache License 2.0 –&lt;/strong&gt; It picks from options or scores against a rubric, and it returns a calibrated confidence with each answer. Its README reports that answers at a confidence of 0.9 or more are right about 95 percent of the time on short classification tasks. Because the model is experimental, validate that result on your own data before you use it in production. You host it yourself, for example on an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; endpoint, by following the &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-labs/strands-decider/tree/main/deploy" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;deployment guides&lt;/a&gt; in its repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A trained classifier on Amazon SageMaker AI –&lt;/strong&gt; It returns class probabilities with low latency and cost once you have labeled training data.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following JSON schema limits a structured-output answer to four queues plus ‘unsure’. Amazon Bedrock requires every object in the schema to set &lt;code&gt;additionalProperties&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;false&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "type": "object",
  "properties": {
    "queue": {
      "type": "string",
      "enum": ["billing", "technical", "account", "other", "unsure"]
    }
  },
  "required": ["queue"],
  "additionalProperties": false
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Whichever option you choose, check its escalation signal against your own labeled data, and set any threshold there. Keep your authorization decisions, such as a refund limit, in a rule.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-deterministic-fast-path-pattern"&gt;The deterministic fast-path pattern&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Many cost patterns focus on routing between models. The fast path goes one level earlier: Run the deterministic check first, and send only what the rule can’t settle to a model. Intent routing is one example. Many messages carry an unambiguous signal, such as “refund” or “reset my password”, which a keyword match resolves in single-digit milliseconds or less. If rules resolve 95 percent of requests, only 1 in 20 requests reaches a model, before any savings from model selection. (Illustrative arithmetic, not a benchmark.)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On AWS, you compose this from existing building blocks:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Step Functions Choice states branch before the workflow invokes any model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Lambda hosts rules that need more than a Choice state, such as validation and schema checks.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon API Gateway (REST API) request validation rejects malformed input before any compute runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A constrained decision call or an Amazon Bedrock model handles only the residual.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following Choice state is a keyword fast path. It uses JSONata to send refund and password-reset messages straight to code, and it sends everything else to the constrained decision call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;"KeywordFastPath": {
  "Type": "Choice",
  "QueryLanguage": "JSONata",
  "Choices": [
    {
      "Condition": "{% $contains($states.input.message, /refund/i) %}",
      "Next": "RefundFlow"
    },
    {
      "Condition": "{% $contains($states.input.message, /reset (my )?password/i) %}",
      "Next": "PasswordResetFlow"
    }
  ],
  "Default": "ConstrainedDecisionCall"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also let a model write the rule once instead of running it every time. Have it draft a regular expression or a JSON schema, review it, and commit it. At runtime the rule runs at rule speed and cost. Review a model-written regular expression, and test it against known good and bad inputs. Avoid nested quantifiers such as &lt;code&gt;(a+)+&lt;/code&gt;, which can make matching very slow on some inputs (regular expression denial of service, or ReDoS).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you do generate, Amazon Bedrock structured outputs constrain a response to a JSON schema that you define. The response then matches the schema, so your code can parse it without custom parsing logic (check content for correctness). Keep range checks, such as numeric bounds and length limits, in code. For supported models and schema features, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/structured-output.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Get validated JSON results from models&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your rules live in configuration, allow only known primitives, such as regular expressions, schema checks, keyword lists, and range checks. Never run arbitrary code from a configuration file.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="when-you-do-need-a-model-right-size-it"&gt;When you do need a model, right-size it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose the cheapest and fastest model that meets your quality floor. That is often not the newest or largest model. The Generative AI Lens covers this in its best practice on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/generative-ai-lens/gencost01-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;right-sizing model selection&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/effective-cost-optimization-strategies-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Effective cost optimization strategies for Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; covers it in depth. Define the requirements before you compare models:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Quality floor:&lt;/strong&gt; The minimum acceptable output quality, measured on a golden evaluation set (labeled examples of the task with known-correct answers).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost ceiling:&lt;/strong&gt; The most you pay per request or per successful outcome.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency target:&lt;/strong&gt; The p95 or p99 response time the experience can tolerate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compliance constraints:&lt;/strong&gt; Requirements such as data residency that can rule out candidates.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock offers many model families and sizes behind one API, so you can right-size each task without changing your integration. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-routing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Intelligent Prompt Routing&lt;/a&gt; routes each request to a suitable model within a supported model family. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-distillation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Model Distillation&lt;/a&gt; transfers knowledge from a larger model to a smaller, cheaper one for your use case. Model providers evaluate a new frontier model on broad benchmarks, but your workload is one task with one quality floor. A larger model can add cost and latency without improving that task. Before you switch, evaluate each launch against your requirements, such as quality on your golden evaluation set and your latency target.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="treat-model-choice-as-tested-configuration"&gt;Treat model choice as tested configuration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Generative AI Lens recommends testing candidate models against your ground truth data and having a way to update the model quickly. Go further and treat the decision as a tested configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Keep a golden evaluation set per task, with labeled ground truth, and run it in continuous integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Gate on your requirements. Treat a cheaper model that drops below the quality floor as a regression.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Put the model behind a configuration flag so that you can reverse a swap.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Verify with the right oracle. Check rules against ground truth. Reserve model-as-judge evaluation for open-ended outputs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Watch for drift. Sample some rule-resolved requests through a model in shadow mode, and reclassify a step when they disagree.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="putting-it-into-practice"&gt;Putting it into practice&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Spend model capacity where judgment is needed, and nowhere else. You can get there in four steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instrument –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Tag every call as deterministic code, a constrained decision call, or a model call. Track cost per successful outcome.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Move the rules to code –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Run rule-checkable steps on AWS Lambda or AWS Step Functions. This is usually the largest and safest win.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Add constrained decision calls –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Route fixed-choice steps through a call that escalates when it is unsure.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Test before you switch models –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Run every new model through your golden evaluation set before it becomes the default.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learned how to decide whether a workflow step needs a model at all. Rules belong in deterministic code, and fixed choices belong in a constrained decision call. Open-ended work goes to a right-sized Amazon Bedrock model. Aim to meet each requirement at the lowest cost and latency that your quality floor allows. Ask whether a step needs a model before you ask which one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more information, refer to these posts on the AWS Artificial Intelligence Blog:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/use-decision-models-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-evaluations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Use decision models with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/use-amazon-bedrock-intelligent-prompt-routing-for-cost-and-latency-benefits/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Use Amazon Bedrock Intelligent Prompt Routing for cost and latency benefits&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/structured-outputs-on-amazon-bedrock-schema-compliant-ai-responses/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Structured outputs on Amazon Bedrock: Schema-compliant AI responses&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/fix-evaluation-bias-to-unlock-better-models-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Fix evaluation bias to unlock better models on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To try a constrained decision call, open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt;, or deploy Strands Decider from its &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-labs/strands-decider" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start with one high-volume workflow and measure how much of it never needed a model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
