			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Secure OAuth patterns for Snowflake Cortex AI through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/secure-oauth-patterns-for-snowflake-cortex-ai-through-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Nithyashree Alwarsamy]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:25:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">9736c230ccaae3f0cbc3f54ebe3678c8b1d6632f</guid>

					<description>When an AI agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore calls Snowflake Cortex AI over MCP, how does it authenticate? This post walks through three OAuth patterns we tested on AgentCore Identity: Snowflake OAuth, three-legged OAuth with a corporate IdP, and two-legged OAuth, so you can match your organization's identity, audit, and compliance needs.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Enterprises today need a clear way to extend their authentication and authorization controls into the agentic layer, often requiring either broad service account permissions or custom-built auth integrations that add operational overhead. When an AI agent running on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; (a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model) needs to call the &lt;a href="https://docs.snowflake.com/en/user-guide/snowflake-cortex/cortex-agents-mcp" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Snowflake-managed MCP server&lt;/a&gt; (which exposes &lt;a href="https://docs.snowflake.com/en/user-guide/snowflake-cortex/cortex-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Snowflake Cortex Agents&lt;/a&gt; as one of its tool types) through the &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol&lt;/a&gt; (MCP), the first question enterprises must answer is: how does the agent authenticate? In this post, we walk through three Open Authorization (OAuth) configurations we tested on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore: Snowflake OAuth (per-user, no external Identity Provider, or IdP), three-legged OAuth with a third-party Identity Provider (corporate single sign-on, or SSO), and two-legged OAuth with a third-party Identity Provider (shared service user). Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity also supports an on-behalf-of (OBO) grant (TOKEN_EXCHANGE) that delivers per-user identity for unattended workloads without a consent prompt. We focus on the three configurations we tested end-to-end, flagging where OBO fits as we go. By the end, you will be able to identify which pattern suits your enterprise scenario.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, connects AI agents to external tool providers through MCP server targets. It targets the Snowflake-managed MCP server, which surfaces Cortex Search and Cortex Analyst as MCP tools that an MCP-aware client can discover and invoke. Rather than acting as a wrapper, the Gateway aggregates the capabilities of its configured targets into a single unified catalog, which it presents to the agent as one tools/list response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The core focus of this post is the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-outbound-auth.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;outbound authentication&lt;/a&gt; for Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway: how it obtains and presents OAuth tokens to the Snowflake-managed MCP server. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity acts as the credential broker: the Gateway obtains a workload token by calling GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT (because the Gateway uses JSON Web Token (JWT) inbound authentication), then calls GetResourceOauth2Token against the configured provider. AgentCore Identity owns the token vault, the token refresh, and the user-to-token binding, and it fetches, caches, and refreshes tokens automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity supports three OAuth configurations spanning two grant types (authorization_code and client_credentials):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.snowflake.com/en/user-guide/oauth-snowflake-overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Snowflake OAuth&lt;/a&gt;: Snowflake acts as its own OAuth authorization server. Each user authenticates directly to Snowflake through the Authorization Code grant (3LO). No external identity provider is needed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Three-legged OAuth using a third-party Identity Provider: Users authenticate through corporate SSO with the Authorization Code grant. Snowflake trusts IdP-issued JSON Web Tokens (JWTs) through its &lt;a href="https://docs.snowflake.com/en/user-guide/oauth-ext-overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;External OAuth&lt;/a&gt; security integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Two-legged OAuth pattern using a third-party IdP: A shared service user authenticates through the client_credentials grant of the IdP. No user interaction or consent is required, which is ideal for machine-to-machine workloads.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity also offers an API-key credential provider (which supports Snowflake Programmatic Access Tokens). We focus on OAuth here for per-user attribution and automatic token refresh. Each configuration uses a credential provider in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity (CustomOauth2, OktaOauth2, or MicrosoftOauth2) and a corresponding security integration on the Snowflake side.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; We tested these patterns with the Snowflake-managed MCP server using Okta and Microsoft Entra ID. Patterns 2 and 3 (external IdP) follow standard OAuth flows, so they can extend to other OAuth-protected MCP servers, but validate the token claims, scopes, and audience settings for your SaaS provider. Only Pattern 1 is Snowflake-specific, because it relies on Snowflake’s built-in OAuth authorization server. The Snowflake-specific elements are the OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER issuer-lock parameter, the session:role: / session:scope: grammar, BLOCKED_ROLES_LIST, LOGIN_NAME / EMAIL_ADDRESS mapping, and QUERY_HISTORY attribution. If your SaaS exposes its own internal OAuth server, you configure outbound OAuth the same way as Snowflake OAuth.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pattern&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;OAuth grant&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Identity credential provider vendor&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Snowflake security integration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Snowflake query identity mapping&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Snowflake OAuth – 3LO&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;authorization_code&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Custom provider (CustomOauth2)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TYPE = OAUTH, OAUTH_CLIENT = CUSTOM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The real user, with their assigned role&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3LO with external IdP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;authorization_code&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Built-in provider (such as OktaOauth2, MicrosoftOauth2)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TYPE = EXTERNAL_OAUTH + OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER (set with ALTER ACCOUNT / DATABASE / SCHEMA); EXTERNAL_OAUTH_TYPE = OKTA / AZURE / PING_FEDERATE / CUSTOM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The real user, with their assigned role&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2LO with external IdP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;client_credentials&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Built-in provider (such as OktaOauth2, MicrosoftOauth2) or CustomOauth2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TYPE = EXTERNAL_OAUTH + OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER (set with ALTER ACCOUNT / DATABASE / SCHEMA); EXTERNAL_OAUTH_TYPE = OKTA / AZURE / PING_FEDERATE / CUSTOM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A shared Snowflake service user&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Notes:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;2LO does not require a built-in vendor. CustomOauth2 with the CLIENT_CREDENTIALS grant can work with your choice of OAuth 2.0 authorization server.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway brokers the token (the pattern in this post), Snowflake validates it through the TYPE = EXTERNAL_OAUTH security integration. Setting OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER (with ALTER ACCOUNT, DATABASE, or SCHEMA) is what lets direct MCP clients auto-discover your IdP through the Protected Resource Metadata endpoint, and it issuer-locks the MCP endpoint to that integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If you set OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER to issuer-lock an MCP scope to an external IdP (Pattern 2), Snowflake OAuth tokens (Pattern 1) are rejected for MCP servers in that scope and do not fall back. If you need both patterns in one account, set this parameter per schema or database so each scope resolves to the right authorization server.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-architecture"&gt;Solution architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the solution we built and the components it uses: a user-facing AI application, a &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands agent&lt;/a&gt; on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; for reasoning, with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; redacting personally identifiable information (PII) from prompts and responses, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;, the fully managed Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) capability, over policy documents in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway with the Snowflake-managed MCP server as its target, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity holding the token vault. The outbound OAuth path has the same shape in all three patterns: the Gateway resolves a token through AgentCore Identity, which obtains and renews it from an authorization server, and the Gateway then presents that token to the Snowflake-managed MCP server. What changes between the patterns is which authorization server issues the token and under which grant: Snowflake’s own built-in OAuth server, or a third-party IdP. Those differences are set out in the Pattern comparison table, and each pattern’s runtime flow has its own sequence diagram later in this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-1-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-1-2.png" alt="Architecture diagram: users reach an AI application that invokes a Strands agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, which uses Amazon Bedrock with Guardrails and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases and calls Snowflake Cortex AI tools through AgentCore Gateway. AgentCore Identity obtains tokens from Snowflake’s built-in OAuth server or a third-party identity provider." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Solution overview: the reference agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, combining Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases with Snowflake Cortex AI through the Snowflake-managed MCP server, and the authorization server that differs across the three OAuth patterns&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="pattern-analysis"&gt;Pattern analysis&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections examine each pattern in detail: the authentication flow, configuration steps, and trade-offs to help you select the right approach for your use case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pattern-1-snowflake-oauth-three-legged"&gt;Pattern 1: Snowflake OAuth (three-legged)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Snowflake OAuth pattern uses a three-legged OAuth (3LO) flow to deliver per-user authentication without requiring an external identity provider. Each user authenticates to Snowflake with their own credentials, consents to a specific Snowflake role, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity caches that user’s access and refresh tokens. Every subsequent Snowflake call executes as that user, and QUERY_HISTORY records their real username. Note that per-role consent is not the default. The Snowflake-managed MCP server advertises session:role:all and falls back to DEFAULT_ROLE, so you set OAUTH_SCOPES_SUPPORTED (an account-, database-, or schema-level parameter set with ALTER) to narrow the role scope the server advertises, and restrict ALLOWED_ROLES_LIST on the integration to enforce which roles a user can consent to (PRE_AUTHORIZED_ROLES_LIST does the opposite: it lists roles that skip the consent step).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What makes this pattern appealing is its simplicity: no external identity provider is required. Snowflake acts as both the authorization server and the resource server, exposing its own built-in OAuth endpoints (/oauth/authorize and /oauth/token-request). Setup follows a specific order, because the callback URL does not exist until the credential provider is created. The administrator first creates the CustomOauth2 credential provider in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity with placeholder client credentials and records the callbackUrl that AgentCore Identity returns. That callbackUrl is then registered as the redirect URI on a Snowflake security integration created with TYPE = OAUTH and OAUTH_CLIENT = CUSTOM. Finally, the administrator updates the credential provider with the real client ID and secret issued by Snowflake and configures the provider’s oauthDiscovery with explicit authorization server metadata: the issuer (the Snowflake account URL), the authorization endpoint (/oauth/authorize), and the token endpoint (/oauth/token-request).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the configuration steps for credential provider vendors in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity with Snowflake OAuth (3LO) settings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-2-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-2-2.png" alt="AgentCore console showing a Custom OAuth2 credential provider for Snowflake, with its callback URL, client ID, and Snowflake authorization and token endpoints." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Configuring the Snowflake OAuth (3LO) credential provider vendor in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next, the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway configuration points to the Snowflake-managed MCP server endpoint and references the custom Snowflake OAuth for outbound authentication.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-3-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-3-2.png" alt="AgentCore console showing the Snowflake MCP server gateway target in Ready status, with outbound auth using the Snowflake OAuth provider and the session:role:ANALYST_ROLE scope." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway configuration pointing to the Snowflake-managed MCP server endpoint and Snowflake OAuth (3LO) as outbound auth&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sequence diagram shows the runtime authorization flow for the Snowflake OAuth pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-4-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-4-2.png" alt="Sequence diagram of the Snowflake OAuth 3LO runtime flow: the Gateway returns a -32042 elicitation, the user signs in at Snowflake, the app calls CompleteResourceTokenAuth, and the retried tool call runs as the user." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Snowflake OAuth (3LO) runtime authorization flow: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway MCP server target with AUTHORIZATION_CODE grant, per-user Snowflake OAuth, and session:role:ANALYST_ROLE scope&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://docs.snowflake.com/en/user-guide/oauth-snowflake-overview#snowflake-oauth-authorization-flow" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Snowflake OAuth authorization flow&lt;/a&gt; works as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When a tool call requires a Snowflake token, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway obtains a workload token by calling GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT and calls GetResourceOauth2Token to check the token vault of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity for the user. If no token is cached, the Gateway returns a JSON-RPC error code &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/connecting-mcp-servers-to-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-using-authorization-code-flow/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;-32042&lt;/a&gt; carrying a URL elicitation. That authorization URL belongs to AgentCore Identity and is &lt;code&gt;session-URI-bound&lt;/code&gt;. The client opens that URL in the user’s browser, which is routed to Snowflake’s built-in authorization server (/oauth/authorize) and shown a Snowflake login screen.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user enters their Snowflake credentials and is presented with a consent screen asking them to authorize access using a specific Snowflake role (for example, ANALYST_ROLE). Role names in the requested scope must be uppercase (for example, session:role:ANALYST_ROLE).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Upon consent, Snowflake’s authorization server returns an authorization code to the callback URL of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity. AgentCore Identity then redirects the user’s browser to the return URL with the session URI.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore Identity completes the code exchange only after the application calls CompleteResourceTokenAuth with the user identity and session URI (valid for 10 minutes). This step binds the code to the originating request and blocks authorization-URL hijacking. The return URL used here is registered by calling UpdateWorkloadIdentity. In production, the endpoint at that URL is hosted either by your web application or, for gateways with JWT inbound authentication, by the AgentCore Identity Consent Portal (&lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;portal-url&amp;gt;/connect/callback&lt;/code&gt;). The agentcore dev CLI hosts it only for local development. AgentCore Identity then exchanges the code at Snowflake’s token endpoint (/oauth/token-request) for an access token and, optionally, a refresh token.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When the client retries the tool call, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway retrieves the cached access token from AgentCore Identity and presents it to the Snowflake-managed MCP server. Snowflake validates the token and creates a user session scoped to the consented role. The consented scope controls the &lt;strong&gt;primary&lt;/strong&gt; role only. Because secondary roles are governed by the integration, set OAUTH_USE_SECONDARY_ROLES = NONE (the default) so the session cannot carry more privilege than the consented role, which the IMPLICIT setting would allow.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Access tokens are short-lived. By default, they last 600 seconds (maximum 3600). When an access token expires, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity automatically uses the refresh token to obtain a new access token, with no user interaction required. If the security integration is configured to prevent refreshing tokens, the user must re-authorize through the consent screen. Refresh-token lifetime is controlled by OAUTH_REFRESH_TOKEN_VALIDITY. The reference implementation sets it to 86400 seconds (1 day), and Snowflake allows up to 90 days. OAUTH_ISSUE_REFRESH_TOKENS (default TRUE) governs whether refresh tokens are issued at all.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; By default, Snowflake helps prevent the ACCOUNTADMIN, ORGADMIN, GLOBALORGADMIN, and SECURITYADMIN roles from being used with OAuth integrations. This is controlled by BLOCKED_ROLES_LIST for Snowflake OAuth (and EXTERNAL_OAUTH_BLOCKED_ROLES_LIST for External OAuth), applies to all three patterns, and covers the primary role only. With OAUTH_USE_SECONDARY_ROLES = IMPLICIT, secondary roles can still carry these privileged roles.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="considerations"&gt;Considerations&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Snowflake OAuth pattern is the simplest path to per-user audit trails. It needs no external IdP configuration and no additional vendor dependency. It is best suited for scenarios where:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Users already have Snowflake credentials and are comfortable signing in directly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You want per-user attribution in QUERY_HISTORY without an external identity provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You are in a proof-of-concept phase and want to validate the per-user model quickly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Compliance requirements mandate individual accountability, so each query must be traceable to a specific user, not a shared service user.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You prefer to manage user lifecycle (provisioning, deprovisioning, role grants) entirely within Snowflake.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You want to avoid token claim mapping complexities. Snowflake issues tokens and knows exactly which user they represent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pattern-2-three-legged-oauth-3lo-using-a-third-party-identity-provider-idp"&gt;Pattern 2: Three-legged OAuth (3LO) using a third-party Identity Provider (IdP)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This pattern combines per-user authentication with corporate single sign-on. Users sign in through the external IdP, which enforces multi-factor authentication (MFA), conditional access, and session policies. The IdP-issued JWT is presented to Snowflake, which validates it through its &lt;a href="https://docs.snowflake.com/en/user-guide/oauth-ext-custom" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;External OAuth security integration&lt;/a&gt; (optionally issuer-locked to the MCP server’s schema, database, or account with OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER). Snowflake verifies the issuer, audience, and signature through a JSON Web Key Set (JWKS). It then maps a configurable claim, set with EXTERNAL_OAUTH_TOKEN_USER_MAPPING_CLAIM (sub for &lt;a href="https://www.okta.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Okta&lt;/a&gt;, upn for &lt;a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/business/microsoft-entra" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Microsoft Entra ID&lt;/a&gt;), to a Snowflake user’s LOGIN_NAME or EMAIL_ADDRESS (with EXTERNAL_OAUTH_SNOWFLAKE_USER_MAPPING_ATTRIBUTE), and runs the query as that user. The security integration parameter values are case-sensitive.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key difference from Pattern 1: the authorization server is your corporate IdP (such as Okta, Microsoft Entra ID), not Snowflake. Snowflake remains the resource server. Users authenticate with the same credentials and policies they use for every other corporate application. No separate Snowflake passwords are required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore side, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity provides built-in credential provider vendors for popular identity providers (for example, OktaOauth2 and MicrosoftOauth2). These vendors accept the client ID, client secret, and IdP endpoints through includedOauth2ProviderConfig, compared to the generic CustomOauth2 provider used in Pattern 1. The Snowflake-managed MCP server uses pre-registered OAuth clients, so you register each client ID and secret in advance, which is why the create, record callbackUrl, register, update sequence is required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-5-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-5-2.png" alt="AgentCore console showing an Okta OAuth2 credential provider, with its callback URL, client ID, and Okta authorization and token endpoints." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Configuring the Okta OAuth (3-legged) credential provider vendor in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next, the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway configuration points to the Snowflake-managed MCP server endpoint and references the Okta credential provider for outbound authentication.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-6-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-6-2.png" alt="AgentCore console showing the Okta 3LO gateway target in Ready status, with outbound auth using the Okta provider and the session:role:ANALYST_ROLE scope." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway configuration pointing to the Snowflake-managed MCP server endpoint with Okta 3-legged OAuth as outbound authentication&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sequence diagram shows the runtime authorization flow for the three-legged OAuth pattern with an external IdP.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-7-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-7-2.png" alt="Sequence diagram of the 3LO flow with an external identity provider: the user signs in through Okta or Microsoft Entra ID, AgentCore Identity stores the token after CompleteResourceTokenAuth, and Snowflake validates it through its External OAuth integration." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: 3LO with external IdP runtime authorization flow: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway MCP server target with AUTHORIZATION_CODE grant and third-party IdP outbound auth&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Snowflake’s External OAuth security integration flow works as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When a tool call requires a Snowflake token, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway obtains a workload token by calling GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT and calls GetResourceOauth2Token to check the token vault of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity for the user. If no token is cached, the Gateway returns a JSON-RPC error code -32042 carrying a URL elicitation. That authorization URL belongs to AgentCore Identity and is &lt;code&gt;session-URI-bound&lt;/code&gt;, not the IdP’s. The client opens that URL in the user’s browser, and AgentCore Identity routes the user to the IdP.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user authenticates through the third-party IdP with corporate SSO (including MFA) and consents to the requested scopes. For Okta, the role scope takes the form session:role:ANALYST_ROLE. Microsoft Entra ID delegated flows use session:scope: instead.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The IdP returns an authorization code to the callback URL of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity, and AgentCore Identity redirects the user’s browser to the return URL with the session URI. Only after the application calls CompleteResourceTokenAuth to validate the user identity and session URI does AgentCore Identity exchange the code for an access token (and a refresh token, if the IdP issues one) and cache them in the per-user token vault.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When the client retries the tool call (and on later calls), Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway retrieves the cached IdP-issued JWT from Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity and presents it to the Snowflake-managed MCP server. Snowflake validates the token through its External OAuth integration: it verifies the JWKS signature and audience (aud) claim, maps the configured user claim (sub for Okta, upn for Microsoft Entra ID) to the Snowflake user, and executes the query as the authenticated user. Snowflake does not call the IdP at runtime. It validates tokens locally using the IdP’s published JWKS signing keys.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When the access token expires, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity automatically refreshes it using the cached refresh token, if one was issued (for Okta, request the offline_access scope). No user interaction is required until the refresh token expires.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Token lifetime governance: Unlike Pattern 1 where Snowflake controls token lifetimes, access token and refresh token lifetimes are governed by the IdP, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity handles the refresh transparently. If a refresh token was issued, the user re-authenticates only when it expires, based on the IdP’s session policy. Otherwise, the user re-authenticates when the access token expires.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="considerations-1"&gt;Considerations&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose three-legged OAuth (3LO) with a third-party IdP when:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Your organization or security team requires all authentication to flow through corporate SSO for centralized MFA, conditional access, and session management.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You want to use existing IdP features such as group-based role mapping, session timeout policies, and geo-based conditional access rules.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You need per-user audit trails in Snowflake’s QUERY_HISTORY.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You want uniform security policy enforcement across all applications, including AI agents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You need centralized access control. Disabling a user in the IdP helps prevent new Snowflake authorizations, though cached tokens stay valid until they expire. To force a user to re-authorize, the application can pass forceAuthentication=true on the next request, which clears the stored refresh token and starts a new authorization flow. To revoke access outright, revoke the grant at the IdP or in Snowflake.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pattern-3-two-legged-oauth-2lo-using-a-third-party-identity-provider-idp"&gt;Pattern 3: Two-legged OAuth (2LO) using a third-party Identity Provider (IdP)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two-legged OAuth pattern uses the OAuth 2.0 client_credentials grant. This is a machine-to-machine flow with no user interaction. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity obtains a JWT from the IdP using the configured client ID and client secret, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway presents it to Snowflake, which executes the requests under a shared service user, regardless of which end user initiated the query.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This pattern is the most operationally direct: no consent screens, no per-user token vaults, no browser redirects. The users follow the same code path. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity fetches and caches the client_credentials token automatically, and the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway presents it on every outbound call to the Snowflake-managed MCP server.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key difference from Patterns 1 and 2: there is no per-user identity propagation. Snowflake’s QUERY_HISTORY records a single service user for queries. The trade-off is operational simplicity at the cost of per-user audit granularity. On the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore side, the credential provider uses the client_credentials grant type: either a built-in vendor (OktaOauth2, MicrosoftOauth2) or the generic CustomOauth2 provider. Configuration requires the IdP’s token endpoint, client ID, and client secret. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity fetches a token from the IdP on the first tool call (when the Gateway calls GetResourceOauth2Token), caches it, and, because client_credentials issues no refresh token, requests a fresh one before expiry. No agent-level code changes are required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-8-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-8-2.png" alt="AgentCore console showing a Custom OAuth2 credential provider for the client credentials flow, with the Okta token endpoint and a token response type." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: Configuring the client_credentials (2-legged) credential provider vendor in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next, the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway configuration references the Snowflake-managed MCP server endpoint and the two-legged OAuth credential provider for outbound authentication.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-9-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-9-2.png" alt="AgentCore console showing the 2LO gateway target in Ready status, with outbound auth using the client credentials provider and the session:role:MCP_GATEWAY_ROLE scope." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway configuration pointing to the Snowflake-managed MCP server endpoint with 2-legged OAuth (client_credentials) as outbound authentication&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sequence diagram shows the runtime authorization flow for the two-legged OAuth pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-10-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21095-10-2.png" alt="Sequence diagram of the 2LO client credentials flow: AgentCore Identity requests a token from Okta or Microsoft Entra ID with no user step, and Snowflake validates it and runs the query as a shared service user." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 10: 2LO with external IdP runtime authorization flow: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway MCP server target with CLIENT_CREDENTIALS grant and third-party IdP outbound auth&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Snowflake’s External OAuth security integration 2LO flow works as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When a tool call requires a Snowflake token, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway obtains a workload token by calling GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT and calls GetResourceOauth2Token on the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity credential provider. If a valid token is cached, Identity returns it immediately.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity calls the third-party IdP’s token endpoint using the client_credentials grant with the configured client ID and client secret. For Okta, the requested scope includes the Snowflake role (for example, session:role:MCP_GATEWAY_ROLE). For Microsoft Entra ID, the client requests the api:&lt;code&gt;//&amp;lt;application-id&amp;gt;/.default&lt;/code&gt; scope and the role arrives in the roles claim from an app role whose value is session:role:MCP_GATEWAY_ROLE.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The IdP returns an access token (a JWT, with no refresh token) whose aud claim is the Snowflake account URL or an additional value listed in EXTERNAL_OAUTH_AUDIENCE_LIST (for Microsoft Entra ID, typically the Application ID URI). For Okta, the sub claim is the client ID. For Microsoft Entra ID, the client ID is carried in appid/azp while sub holds the service principal’s object ID (a GUID), so with Entra, LOGIN_NAME must map to that GUID.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway presents the JWT to the Snowflake-managed MCP server. Snowflake validates the token through its External OAuth integration (optionally issuer-locked with OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER, as in Pattern 2). It verifies the signature through EXTERNAL_OAUTH_JWS_KEYS_URL (or EXTERNAL_OAUTH_RSA_PUBLIC_KEY), the issuer through EXTERNAL_OAUTH_ISSUER, and the aud claim against the Snowflake account URL plus any additional values in EXTERNAL_OAUTH_AUDIENCE_LIST (these security integration parameter values are case-sensitive). Snowflake then maps the sub claim to a Snowflake service user through LOGIN_NAME and executes the query under that service user’s role. As in Pattern 2, Snowflake validates the token locally and does not call the IdP at runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity caches the token in the token vault (shared across users, not per user). Subsequent calls reuse it, and Identity requests a new token with the client_credentials grant before it expires.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the two-legged OAuth target needs no user consent, the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway connects to the Snowflake-managed MCP server at target-creation time and discovers tools through implicit sync during target creation. In the 3-legged OAuth patterns (Patterns 1 and 2), the Gateway target supports two creation methods: (1) an admin completes the authorization code flow so tools are discovered by implicit sync, or (2) the admin provides the tool schema upfront. (Discovery behavior is a ListingMode property of the target, not a property of the grant type.)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="considerations-2"&gt;Considerations&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two-legged OAuth pattern is preferred for automated, unattended, and batch workloads. It is ideal when:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Your workload is machine-to-machine: scheduled jobs, background pipelines, or system-level agents with no human in the loop.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Per-user identity isn’t required, and a shared service user is acceptable for audit purposes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You need the most direct operational model: no consent flows, per-user token management, or browser redirects.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Gateway can connect to Snowflake and discover MCP tools by implicit sync at target-creation time, without waiting for user interaction.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;All users share the same data access rights under a single Snowflake role.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Accept these trade-offs:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Because every request runs as the shared service user, Snowflake’s row-level security and column masking policies apply that user’s permissions to requests. Policies keyed on CURRENT_USER() and CURRENT_ROLE() all resolve to that single service user and its one role, which is the strongest case for the on-behalf-of (OBO) approach.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;There is no user-level token revocation. To cut off access, revoke the client credentials at the IdP or disable the Snowflake security integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If credentials are compromised, agent access is affected. Rotate client secrets regularly and restrict the service user to least-privilege access.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="pattern-comparison"&gt;Pattern comparison&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Aspect&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pattern 1: Snowflake OAuth (3LO)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pattern 2: 3LO with external IdP&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pattern 3: 2LO with external IdP&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OAuth grant type&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;authorization_code&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;authorization_code&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;client_credentials&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore Identity provider&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Custom provider (CustomOauth2)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Built-in provider (for example, OktaOauth2 or MicrosoftOauth2)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Built-in provider (for example, OktaOauth2, MicrosoftOauth2) or CustomOauth2&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Authorization Server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Snowflake built-in OAuth&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Third-party IdP (for example, Okta, Entra ID)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Third-party IdP (for example, Okta, Entra ID)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Identity visible to Snowflake&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The real user&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The real user&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Shared service user&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;RBAC Model&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-user Snowflake role&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-user Snowflake role&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Single role for requests&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Audit granularity (QUERY_HISTORY)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Real username&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Real username&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Service user&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;External IdP required&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Snowflake security integration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TYPE = OAUTH, OAUTH_CLIENT = CUSTOM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TYPE = EXTERNAL_OAUTH + OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER (set with ALTER ACCOUNT / DATABASE / SCHEMA)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TYPE = EXTERNAL_OAUTH + OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER (set with ALTER ACCOUNT / DATABASE / SCHEMA)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;User login experience&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Snowflake login + consent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Corporate SSO&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;None (no user interaction)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MFA / conditional access&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per Snowflake account settings&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Enforced by IdP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NA&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MCP protocol revision¹&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2025-11-25&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2025-11-25&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2025-11-25 (not pinned on the 2LO gateway)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Token lifetime governance&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Snowflake security integration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;IdP authorization server policy&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;IdP authorization server policy&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Works unattended (scheduled jobs)²&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Only until the refresh token expires (reference app: 1 day, up to 90 days)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Only until the refresh token expires (per IdP session policy)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes: no user interaction needed.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Best for&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;POCs needing per-user audit without an IdP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Enterprise apps with existing corporate SSO&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Internal agents, batch jobs, service-to-service&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;¹ MCP protocol revision: for the three-legged patterns (Patterns 1 and 2), the gateway pins 2025-11-25 in its supported MCP revisions, which the URL-mode elicitation (-32042) handshake requires. The reference 2LO gateway (Pattern 3) does not pin it, because 2LO has no elicitation step. On accounts that are not enabled for MCP version updates, the supported set is fixed when the gateway is created.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;² Works unattended: Patterns 1 and 2 run unattended only until the refresh token expires. Re-consent then requires a live browser, the same user, and completion inside the 10-minute authorization window (CompleteResourceTokenAuth), which is why 3LO is unsuitable for fully unattended scheduled jobs. Pattern 3 (2LO) has no refresh token and no consent step, so it runs unattended indefinitely.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The three configurations in this post cover two grant types: authorization_code (Patterns 1 and 2) and client_credentials (Pattern 3). Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity also supports on-behalf-of (OBO) token exchange (TOKEN_EXCHANGE), which delivers per-user identity for unattended workloads &lt;em&gt;without&lt;/em&gt; a consent prompt.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OBO exchanges an inbound user token for a Snowflake-scoped token that preserves the real user’s identity, so QUERY_HISTORY attributes each query to the actual user, while keeping the unattended, machine-to-machine flow of Pattern 3 (no browser redirect, no consent screen). On the Snowflake side it uses the same TYPE = EXTERNAL_OAUTH integration as Patterns 2 and 3 (optionally issuer-locked with OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER). On the AgentCore side it is configured on the OAuth credential provider through onBehalfOfTokenExchangeConfig (grant mode TOKEN_EXCHANGE for Okta/RFC 8693, or JWT_AUTHORIZATION_GRANT for Microsoft Entra). OBO uses the External OAuth path (Patterns 2 and 3).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OBO is generally available on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity, but it was outside the scope of the three configurations we tested for this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="try-it-yourself"&gt;Try it yourself&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To see these patterns in action, we built a financial-services credit-risk assessment agent as a reference workload. A single agent combines bank policy documents in Amazon S3 (through Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases) with customer data in Snowflake. It assesses loan eligibility, searches customer profiles, and queries account and transaction data. The demo exercises three MCP tools (Cortex Search, Cortex Analyst, and SQL Execution) across all three OAuth configurations for side-by-side comparison in your own AWS account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Though the demo is financial services, the patterns work across healthcare, supply chain, insurance, and more. All three implementations are public and deploy end-to-end through a single &lt;code&gt;deploy.sh&lt;/code&gt; script:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Snowflake OAuth (3LO):&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/aws-generativeai-partner-samples/tree/main/snowflake/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-cortex-mcp/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-mcp-gateway-3lo" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-mcp-gateway-3lo&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3LO with external IdP:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/aws-generativeai-partner-samples/tree/main/snowflake/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-cortex-mcp/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-mcp-gateway-3lo-okta" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-mcp-gateway-3lo-okta&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2LO with external IdP:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/aws-generativeai-partner-samples/tree/main/snowflake/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-cortex-mcp/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-mcp-gateway" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-mcp-gateway&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; For demonstration purposes, the reference implementations expose SYSTEM_EXECUTE_SQL on the same MCP server as the Cortex Agent. Snowflake recommends exposing a Cortex Agent as the only client-facing tool for governed business questions. If direct SQL is required, place it on a separate MCP server with a dedicated least-privileged role.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walked through three OAuth configurations for authenticating AI agents to Snowflake Cortex AI over MCP through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway. Snowflake OAuth offers the simplest path to per-user audit trails with no external IdP dependency. Three-legged OAuth with an external IdP adds corporate SSO, centralized MFA, and conditional access while preserving per-user identity. Two-legged OAuth with an external IdP serves machine-to-machine through a shared service user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Across all three patterns, the agent code and the Snowflake MCP tool set stay unchanged. The OAuth wiring differs in four specific ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The grant type flips: authorization_code for Patterns 1 and 2, client_credentials for Pattern 3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AUTHORIZATION_CODE targets need a return URL, set as defaultReturnUrl on the target and optionally overridden per request. 2LO targets need none.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;3LO target creation requires implicit sync or an upfront tool schema, whereas a 2LO target syncs its tools during target creation with no user authorization step.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;OAUTH_AUTHORIZATION_SERVER can be set for Patterns 2 and 3 to issuer-lock an MCP scope to the external IdP and to let direct MCP clients auto-discover it. It is left unset for Pattern 1. When it is set, Patterns 1 and 2 cannot share one scope, as noted earlier.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond these four, only the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity credential provider (CustomOauth2, OktaOauth2, or MicrosoftOauth2) and the Snowflake security integration differ. Understanding these differences in grant type, token issuance, user mapping, and audit behavior equips you to select the right OAuth configuration for your compliance, operational, and user-experience needs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, explore the reference implementation in our &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/aws-generativeai-partner-samples/tree/main/snowflake/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-snowflake-cortex-mcp" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. For more on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore service detail page&lt;/a&gt; or the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/what-is-bedrock-agentcore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Announcing AI data preparation capabilities in Amazon SageMaker: Build high-quality datasets for model development workflows</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/announcing-ai-data-preparation-capabilities-in-amazon-sagemaker-build-high-quality-datasets-for-model-development-workflows/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Varun Rajan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 19:40:01 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">2164f47eaa11e3846a14bf344a027c68fab81f78</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker AI now offers AI data preparation capabilities for building high-quality training and evaluation data. Generate synthetic examples, assess and improve datasets, auto-label at scale, and bring in expert review through Python SDK APIs and an agent skill, all from your own notebook or IDE.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Teams that ship generative AI to production know that a model is only as good as its data. That is why preparing high-quality training and evaluation data comes first. Fine-tuning an open-weight model, running reinforcement learning, or building an evaluation suite all depend on data that is accurate, well-labeled, and representative of your task. For narrow, well-defined tasks, a smaller model customized on clean, task-specific data can often match or exceed the accuracy of a larger general-purpose model prompted into shape, at a lower cost per inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In practice, though, preparing that data is often the slowest part of the project. Labeled examples are scarce or expensive, and privacy rules limit what you can use. Quality problems such as duplicates, label noise, and answer leakage go unnoticed until a training run disappoints, and every training technique expects data in its own format. As a result, data scientists spend weeks wrangling data instead of building models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, we’re expanding Amazon SageMaker AI with new AI data preparation capabilities for model development workflows. You can generate synthetic examples, assess and improve existing datasets, and bring expert feedback into the process through Python SDK APIs and the AWS AI-ML agent skill. You work in your notebook or IDE, with specifications and code that you can inspect and change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prepare-data-without-hand-building-pipelines"&gt;Prepare data without hand-building pipelines&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With AI Data Preparation capabilities, you don’t need to stitch together labeling tools, generation scripts, and validation jobs. Each capability is a standalone building block. You can use one of them on its own or chain them together according to the workflow you want.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Auto-labeling:&lt;/strong&gt; Label data at scale from an unlabeled dataset, a small labeled seed set, or no labels at all. Built-in task types include classification, named entity recognition, extractive question answering, ordinal rating, and summarization, and you can define custom tasks. You describe the task in a short labeling spec, which is validated against your own examples before the full dataset is labeled. For tasks with a fixed set of possible answers, such as classification, named entity recognition, and extractive question answering, a jury of several models labels each row, and their level of agreement becomes that row’s confidence score.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human review:&lt;/strong&gt; Rows below your confidence threshold go to a review queue in Amazon SageMaker Studio, which uses the open-source Label Studio Community Edition. Outside subject matter experts can access the review app through the link in the partner app box in SageMaker Studio. Reviewer corrections feed back into auto-labeling, and you can generate consensus metrics and labeling reports.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Synthetic data generation:&lt;/strong&gt; Generate training, evaluation, and test data from a specification. You can ground the output in your own seed data and documents, and the service generates a code-based data generation pipeline that you can review, modify, reuse, and scale.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data quality evaluation:&lt;/strong&gt; Check datasets against your own business rules, built-in metrics, and LLM-as-a-judge metrics. Find duplicates and near-duplicates, and surface issues you didn’t think to check for, such as label noise or answer leakage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data transformation:&lt;/strong&gt; Clean and reshape data for your target training technique. Describe the change in plain language and have the code generated for you, use built-in operations such as dedupe, merge, split, and filter, or run your own Python at scale.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You work wherever you already work. Use the SageMaker Python SDK directly or provide your coding agent the AI-ML skill in the agent toolkit and let your agent invoke the APIs according to established data science best practices. Describe what you need, and the agent selects and runs the right capability in SageMaker Studio JupyterLab, Kiro, or your local IDE with your coding assistant of choice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For teams building specialized models, the hardest part is often finding enough task-specific data. Splunk, a Cisco company, faced exactly this when building evaluation models for its Agent Observability product.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“For Splunk Agent Observability, our ability to train robust GenAI Eval models relies on access to high-quality, diverse datasets which are hard to curate for very specific tasks. Amazon SageMaker AI data preparation capabilities for synthetic data generation is meaningfully helping us scale these capabilities. We’ve been able to generate statistically representative synthetic datasets reliably and fast, while preserving the underlying patterns our models depend on. The seamless integration with the rest of our SageMaker workflow means our data scientists and customers spend less time wrangling data and more time building models that drive real enterprise value through Splunk.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Yash Sheth, Director of Engineering, Splunk Agent Observability&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="chain-capabilities-from-raw-data-to-training"&gt;Chain capabilities from raw data to training&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The value grows when you combine the capabilities. Some common patterns:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Build a golden evaluation dataset: Auto-label your raw data from a small seed set. High-confidence rows are accepted automatically, and low-confidence rows go to human reviewers. Their corrections improve the next labeling pass, resulting in a trusted, human-validated dataset to benchmark your models against.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Go from raw documents to a trained model in one workflow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generate data:&lt;/strong&gt; Synthetic data generation creates task-specific examples grounded in your documents and seed data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Transform it:&lt;/strong&gt; Data transformation reshapes the data into the format your training technique expects, such as supervised fine-tuning (SFT), direct preference optimization (DPO), or reinforcement learning (RL).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validate it:&lt;/strong&gt; Data quality evaluation checks the dataset for duplicates, leakage, and rule violations before you spend compute on training.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Train:&lt;/strong&gt; Train with SageMaker AI model customization.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider a team preparing data to evaluate agentic customer support assistants. Its product guide says that a factory reset erases saved settings. The team wants to test whether the assistant preserves that warning when answering reset questions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One pass through the preparation workflow looks like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Define the requirements:&lt;/strong&gt; Supply the product guide as context and describe the questions and reference answers you need. Include the requirement that answers about a factory reset preserve the warning about saved settings. Review the specification produced with your coding agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generate and inspect a sample:&lt;/strong&gt; Generate a small batch and check the records against the guide. Review the generation and check the code. Confirm that the examples cover the intended questions and that the reference answers follow the source material.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Assess and review:&lt;/strong&gt; Run a data quality assessment and inspect the record-level findings. Ask a subject matter expert to review ambiguous answers or disputed assessments. Use the results and annotations to request improvement recommendations.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Apply changes and repeat:&lt;/strong&gt; Revise the specification if the requirements need clarification or transform the dataset to address issues such as duplicates. Generate additional examples where needed, then reassess the revised dataset before using it to evaluate the assistant.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This example workflow illustrates how the capabilities fit together.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="keep-control-of-your-preparation-workflow-and-data"&gt;Keep control of your preparation workflow and data&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the AI-ML skill, you describe the task in natural language while your coding agent helps build specifications, prepare code, and coordinate the data preparation steps. You can open those artifacts to see how the workflow is configured and make changes directly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you prefer to invoke the APIs yourself, use the Python SDK APIs to call individual capabilities from your own code. The SDK APIs take a specification as input, so we recommend creating it with the help of an agent. You can add quality assessment to an existing process, generate data for a new task, or combine several operations. You choose the preparation steps your project needs and how they fit into your model customization and evaluation processes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AI Data Preparation runs with an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) execution role that you control. Your source data, generated datasets, and labels stay in your own Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) buckets, in the AWS Region you choose.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started-today"&gt;Get started today&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AI data preparation capabilities are generally available in the US East (N. Virginia) and US West (Oregon) Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are two ways to get started:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With your coding agent –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Set up the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/agent-toolkit-for-aws/tree/main/skills/core-skills/aws-ai-ml/references" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;data preparation skills&lt;/a&gt; in your supported notebook or IDE. Describe one training or evaluation task and review the resulting specification before generating a sample.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With Python –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Use the SageMaker Python SDK directly to run individual data preparation capabilities from your own code. The SDK takes a specification as input, so we recommend creating your specifications with the AI-ML skill first, then using the SDK to run them in your workflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more information, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/ai-data-preparation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; documentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Getting AI out of the demo and into businesses globally</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/getting-ai-out-of-the-demo-and-into-businesses-globally/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dr. Asa Kalavade]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 19:05:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">61e058d52d53c27de0ac686fbb230a20f73cae8c</guid>

					<description>Most enterprises have AI that works in a demo but stalls before production. AWS Forward Deployed Engineering closes that gap. AWS engineers embed with customer teams to take high-value workflows to production in 45-day sprints, leaving a foundation, workflows, and governance the customer owns.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;AWS Forward Deployed Engineering is now available in 15 countries including U.S., Mexico, Brazil, Canada, Australia, Singapore, India, South Korea, Japan, UK, Germany, Netherlands, France, Sweden, and Saudi Arabia for commercial and public sector customers; and powered by dozens of built in production-ready accelerators.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every enterprise I talk to has built something with AI that works. A prototype that reads a claim. An agent that resolves a card dispute. A model that answers a customer question in seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The prototype works. The demo impresses stakeholders. Then the organization tries to put it into production, and the real problems begin. Production data is messier than the test set. Auditors want to know why the agent made each decision. Volume grows from a pilot group to the whole company. The rules that matter most turn out to live in people’s heads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;None of these are model problems. They’re business problems, and they decide whether AI ever pays off. The stretch from demo to production is the last mile, where most AI stalls. It’s also where our teams spend their time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We launched AWS Forward Deployed Engineering (FDE) in June to close that gap. Since then, we’ve worked through these problems with commercial and public sector customers in financial services, healthcare, sports, media, retail, and more. Here’s what we’ve learned, and how it shaped what we built.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-problem-has-three-layers"&gt;The problem has three layers&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model providers, cloud providers, and consulting firms are all embedding engineers with customers. At AWS FDE, we measure success by what remains after our engineers leave: a foundation your team owns, workflows that keep learning, and governance you can trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Across customers, the hardest problems show up in three layers: the foundation, the workflows, and governance. Each one is harder than it looks, and each one builds on the one before it. That holds whether you’re starting fresh or building on existing systems. On a greenfield build, you can move fast, but there’s no foundation to stand on yet. On a brownfield system, agents must work within decades of data, rules, and integrations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="layer-one-a-foundation-that-compounds"&gt;Layer one: A foundation that compounds&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before an agent can do useful work at scale, it needs a foundation: identity, security, context, governance, and operational controls. That foundation must be production-ready from day one and shared by every use case that follows. Context is often the biggest gap. Most customers have data spread across systems and no common ontology, the shared model of how the business works that agents need to reason over.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Without a shared foundation, the second pilot costs nearly as much as the first. Integrations get rebuilt, and lessons fail to carry forward. Most organizations reach a point where they stop and build the foundation first, so each new use case starts from what the last one built instead of from scratch. We call this the AI reset.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Samsung Life Insurance had four affiliates, each building its own AI point solutions from scratch. In 45 days, the FDE team built a governed agentic platform with a single control plane and compliance built in. New agents now deploy through registration and policy assignment instead of a rebuild.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="layer-two-automating-workflows"&gt;Layer two: Automating workflows&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next is the work itself: High-volume, rule-heavy processes such as claims processing, know your customer (KYC) reviews, card disputes, loan origination, invoice reconciliation, permitting, and customer service. This is where AI can deliver immediate and measurable outcomes. The payoff is fast, but the knowledge that drives these processes rarely lives in a system. It lives with the people who do the work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The hard part isn’t automation itself. It is uncovering the business rules behind each decision. When an invoice and a purchase order disagree, which record wins? When can an exception clear on its own, and when does it need a person? Experienced employees answer these questions from judgment, and that judgment is rarely written down.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Sometimes the knowledge is buried in the systems themselves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Rio Tinto Iron Ore runs one of the world’s most complex mine-to-ship supply chains on a 30-year-old Oracle system. That system has more than 5,000 database tables and 140 applications. Mapping it had been a multi-month exercise. The FDE team built agents that mapped it in weeks, surfacing the dependencies that Rio Tinto’s teams could validate and build against.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Mapping what exists is only the start. The biggest opportunities emerge when organizations stop asking how AI can make existing work more efficient and start asking how it can change how work gets done.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="layer-three-governance-that-holds-up-as-everything-changes"&gt;Layer three: Governance that holds up as everything changes&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Governance isn’t a phase of the project. It’s part of the architecture. As agents multiply, so do security risks, costs, and decisions you must explain. Every agent needs its own identity, permissions, and audit trail, separate from human identity. And because the models underneath are probabilistic, agents need monitoring that keeps their results predictable and auditable long after launch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system also must keep working as everything around it changes. Models drift, business rules shift, data formats change, and regulations tighten. New security issues appear, and code falls out of date. As newer models arrive, the instructions and tooling around them will change too. You need evaluations and guardrails that keep agents predictable where they must be and creative where they can be.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When governance comes at the end, it becomes the gate that stops production. When it’s built in from day one, it’s what lets your business move fast.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Marsh, a leading global professional services firm, needed agents that would hold up under its own audit standards. The FDE team built those controls in from the start, and the first use case reached production about two months after work began. Efficiency gains in the targeted areas have been as much as 70 percent, with some process steps moving from hours to seconds. Marsh has since expanded the work and keeps extending the system itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-we-solve-it-speed-scale-and-control"&gt;How we solve it: Speed, scale, and control&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our perspective comes from building and running these systems ourselves. AWS FDE pairs a small team of AWS engineers, scientists, and product managers with your engineers and a crew of specialized AI agents. Many of our FDEs come from the service teams that build Amazon Bedrock, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, Kiro, AWS Quick, and AWS Security Hub. They’ve built and run AI products at AWS scale, and they bring those engineering practices into your environment. Together, our teams take a high-value business problem to production in 45-day sprints, in your own AWS account. Customers shouldn’t need to choose between speed, scale, and control. FDE is built to deliver all three.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Speed –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;You might come to us with a broad goal or an ambiguous problem. We refine it with your business stakeholders, then build in your environment from day one, on your data and through your identity and security controls. We build in short cycles alongside the people who do the work. Each cycle puts real cases in front of them, and the unwritten rules come out as they react. We capture those rules in a living context, so the system keeps learning after we step back. Nothing gets rebuilt between prototype and production. You see working demos throughout, evaluations track progress against the outcomes you care about, and you have a production system within a quarter or less.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scale –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A point solution can move fast on one use case but leaves no foundation for the next five. Every FDE team works the same way, using the AI-Driven Development Lifecycle (AI-DLC), the method our own service teams use to take a broad problem to a working outcome. We deploy an opinionated AWS stack that reflects what we’ve learned building AI services, adapted to your environment: a living context of how your business works, a workbench where your engineers and agent crews build, a runtime on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and a control tower for governance, outcomes, cost, and agent performance. Accelerators for common tasks, such as document processing and customer sentiment analysis, handle the standard pieces, so each new workload can focus on what’s specific to your business. The opinionated AWS stack, context, and controls carry from one use case to the next.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your context is what makes scaling possible. Say your first use case is a sales automation workflow. Because its context lives in your AWS account, the next use case, supply chain optimization, builds on it instead of starting over. Your context, tools, and AWS services all sit in one place, so each use case is faster to build than the last.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Control –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;You own what we build. Everything runs in your AWS account under your identity, network, and security controls. Your engineers work alongside ours from the first sprint, learning both the architecture and the delivery approach on a real business problem. They can then run and extend the solution long after the engagement ends. When we step back, your team runs the system from a control tower, a single view to govern, secure, and monitor every agent. We measure success by how much your team can build without us.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the first or second project, we want you running AI software factories of your own. One financial services customer did exactly that with AI-DLC, with agents and engineers building together. Throughput rose 53%, and code reviews got 56% faster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You also keep your choices open. Models are improving fast. When a better or more cost-effective model arrives, you can adopt it through Amazon Bedrock without rebuilding, so you control both capability and cost. You can also bring your own coding agents. Our opinionated AWS stack, adapted to your environment, connects to your systems through APIs, open standards, and connectors, and it works in hybrid environments. You keep the tools and partners you already use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="a-flywheel-where-everyone-gets-better"&gt;A flywheel where everyone gets better&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your team gets better. Your capability grows with every use case. The living context, the opinionated AWS stack, and your team’s skills carry forward, so the second use case doesn’t rebuild what the first one built.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Services get better. Our engineers bring what they learn from customers back into the products. You build on the latest services, not a custom solution that falls out of date.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All of AWS works around you. Customer development engineering explores early ideas, FDE takes the hardest problems to production, AWS Professional Services and AWS Partners extend the work across your organization, and AWS Support keeps it running. You get the full depth of AWS behind your AI, and you stay in control of how you use it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-new-and-how-to-start"&gt;What’s new, and how to start&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS FDE is now available in 15 countries to commercial and public sector customers, and it’s simpler to buy. Each 45-day sprint has a set price under the FDE Terms and is billed through your AWS account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We work with customers wherever they are in their transformation. For customers starting out, we build a secure, governed foundation and the first workflows on it. For those ready to change how work gets done, we rebuild workflows such as claims processing and KYC reviews, along with the ones their customers use directly, like shopping and issue resolution. Agents then run the work. For those held back by legacy applications, we work with their engineers to build software factories they can reuse for each new workflow. For those already scaling in production, we keep systems secure with agents that find and fix issues, and we lower the cost per outcome.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you’re ready to start, talk with your AWS account team. Bring an executive who owns the outcome and engineers ready to build alongside ours. We’ve learned where projects stall before they start, so our agents take on much of the setup, like access, data, and security checks. Work can begin within days. Then we agree together on what the first sprint will deliver and how we’ll measure success.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gap between AI that works and AI that changes business outcomes is real. It is an engineering, operational, and organizational one, and our teams help customers close it every day. The organizations that succeed with AI won’t necessarily have the most advanced models. They’ll be the ones that build the foundation, workflows, and governance to scale AI across the business.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We’re still early, and we learn something new with every customer. The models will continue to evolve, and so will the tools around them. What should last is a foundation you own, workflows that keep learning, and governance you can trust. That’s what we mean by what remains after our engineers leave.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, contact your AWS account team or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/professional-services/forward-deployed-engineering/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;learn more about AWS FDE&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
