		<item>
		<title>Build WhatsApp AI agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-whatsapp-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Marco Larrea]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 18:48:50 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">ee885e976078b822d4c433d3bc4bee17caaff98f</guid>

					<description>SMART, an open-source Python SDK and Terraform stack, deploys the full WhatsApp channel layer to your AWS account with one command. Bring any agent that runs on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and SMART handles webhook ingestion, media, conversation memory, scheduling, and Meta message templates.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Across Latin America, India, and Southeast Asia, WhatsApp is one of the most widely used messaging applications. That makes it an obvious place to build an AI agent. For many end users, it is how they expect to interact with businesses. However, it is also a genuinely difficult surface to build for, even when your agent already runs on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/what-is-bedrock-agentcore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent is rarely the hard part. The integration is. Inbound messages arrive as deeply nested, type-dependent payloads that must be authenticated and normalized before your agent can read them. Media such as voice notes, images, and documents each require their own processing pipeline. Beyond message handling, you need conversation memory that survives sessions, durable schedules for reminders, and an authenticated path for external systems to trigger outbound messages. And anything you send more than 24 hours after the end user’s last message must use a pre-approved Meta message template.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Wiring that up means configuring more than 10 AWS services and learning a set of WhatsApp system behaviors that most teams discover only by hitting them. None of it is visible to a customer, but all of it has to work. Most teams either build it from scratch or hand the integration to a third-party service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/social-messaging-agent-runtime-toolkit" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SMART (Social Messaging Agent Runtime Toolkit)&lt;/a&gt; is a third option: an open source Python SDK and Terraform stack that gives you the full channel layer. You provide an agent that runs on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, and SMART handles the rest. One command deploys the entire infrastructure to your AWS account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Teams are already running on SMART with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Webmotors, originally scoped as a two-month engagement, compressed delivery to six weeks and moved their internal team into controlled production ahead of a full launch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Whether you are starting from scratch or migrating an existing agent, you own the agent’s logic. You let SMART handle the channel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-channel-layer-you-dont-need-to-build"&gt;The channel layer you don’t need to build&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table compares what each concern costs to build yourself against what SMART provides.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Concern&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Building it yourself&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With SMART&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Webhook ingestion&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Signature verification, nested payloads, a dozen message types&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Handled, so you receive a clean prompt&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Media&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Download, transcribe, store, clean up, and upload outbound files before sending&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Full lifecycle, both directions&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Message templates and the 24-hour window&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Meta approval flow, category rules, tracking which window each conversation is in&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Declared in YAML, synced by CLI&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Conversation memory&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multi-tier memory, session-break handling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore memory, one function call&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scheduling and triggers&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon EventBridge Scheduler wiring, authenticated ingress, routing&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Built in&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Infrastructure&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;More than 10 AWS services stitched together&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One &lt;code&gt;terraform apply&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Local development&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Redeploy and tunnel on every change&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Test locally with the CLI without redeploying&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-agentic-layer"&gt;The agentic layer&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. SMART builds on two of its main capabilities:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;AgentCore runtime&lt;/em&gt; hosts and scales your agent without you managing infrastructure.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;AgentCore memory &lt;/em&gt;persists conversation history across sessions and deployments, scoped to each phone number automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because AgentCore is framework-agnostic, so is SMART. &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/a&gt; gets memory wired automatically. LangGraph, Pydantic-AI, or another framework that gives you control over the conversation loop can integrate too. The SDK exposes the same memory and session hooks regardless of framework. You keep full control over the elements that differentiate your product: framework, model, prompt, and business logic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The contract between SMART and your agent is deliberately small. Your agent receives the inbound message and returns text, optionally with rich message attachments. Everything else, session wiring, memory binding, delivery scheduling, is handled for you.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example is a complete &lt;code&gt;agent.py&lt;/code&gt; for a SMART project. The scaffolding command (&lt;code&gt;socialagent init&lt;/code&gt;) creates the project structure and configuration around it. This file is where your agent logic lives.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# --- AgentCore: runtime entry point ---
from bedrock_agentcore.runtime import BedrockAgentCoreApp
from strands import Agent
from strands.models import BedrockModel
# --- SMART: channel helpers ---
from socialagent_runtime import create_session_manager, socialagent_notify_context, SocialAgentResponse

app = BedrockAgentCoreApp()                                # AgentCore Runtime
model = BedrockModel(model_id="us.anthropic.claude-haiku-5-5")          #Or any Bedrock model

@app.entrypoint
async def invoke(payload: dict, context: object = None) -&amp;gt; SocialAgentResponse:
    agent = Agent(
        model=model,
        system_prompt="You are a helpful WhatsApp assistant. Keep replies short.",
        session_manager=create_session_manager(payload),   # AgentCore Memory, pre-wired
    )
    with socialagent_notify_context() as outbound:         # SMART: collects outbound messages
        result = agent(payload["prompt"], invocation_state={"phone": payload["phone"]})
    return SocialAgentResponse(result=str(result), outbound_messages=outbound)  # SMART: response envelope

app.run()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;create_session_manager()&lt;/code&gt; function binds the agent to AgentCore memory scoped to that phone number, so history survives across sessions and deployments for the memory retention period (7 days by default).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;socialagent_notify_context()&lt;/code&gt; wrapper is the other pattern worth keeping. Tools sometimes need to send something while the agent is still working, such as a button list or a progress update. Sending it immediately corrupts the conversation history, and the user’s next message fails. The wrapper collects those messages instead, and they go out in order once the turn finishes. Your tools send messages normally and do not deal with the timing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how an inbound message travels from WhatsApp to your agent and back, and where the shared infrastructure sits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-21698-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-21698-1.png" alt="SMART architecture showing a WhatsApp message flowing through AWS End User Messaging, AWS Lambda, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore across the message, agent, and service layers" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: How an inbound WhatsApp message flows through the SMART channel layer to the agent and back&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The SMART core stack is organized into distinct layers: message processing, an agent layer, and a service layer. AWS End User Messaging Social receives inbound WhatsApp messages and routes them to the processing layer. An AWS Lambda function handles the channel logic, while Amazon Bedrock AgentCore provides the runtime, memory, and observability for the agent itself. Supporting services such as Amazon DynamoDB (optional, for per-user state), AWS Secrets Manager, Amazon CloudWatch, and Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) supply state management, credentials, monitoring, and storage across the stack.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inbound events arrive through Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS). On each event, the webhook Lambda function verifies the SNS message signature, normalizes the payload, and rate-limits each user (20 messages per minute by default, with the DynamoDB state table enabled). It then fires a read receipt and typing indicator, so the conversation feels alive before the model finishes. Voice notes go through Amazon Transcribe. Other media becomes a descriptor or goes to a hook you register if you want custom handling. When the agent throttles or fails, the user receives a readable message that you can customize, rather than silence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents built on SMART interact directly with end users, so production deployments should include appropriate safeguards. The Terraform stack creates and configures an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; resource by default. With a single &lt;code&gt;guardrail&lt;/code&gt; variable in your environment configuration, you can use the stack’s default guardrail, bring your own, or opt out entirely. The controls that you apply are up to you. SMART makes sure the wiring is already in place.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the message is ready, the webhook function passes it to AgentCore runtime. AgentCore memory maintains conversation context across sessions, and AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, traces every step for debugging and audit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The webhook function sends the reply and messages your tools queued during the turn. A notification function handles sends outside a turn, such as proactive or scheduled messages. External systems trigger your event handlers by posting to an Amazon API Gateway endpoint protected by an API key and a Lambda authorizer. The event is published to Amazon SNS and the event Lambda runs your handler. Amazon EventBridge Scheduler handles time-based outreach, such as reminders and recurring check-ins. With message templates managed through AWS End User Messaging, you can send structured notifications outside the 24-hour WhatsApp session window.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A single &lt;code&gt;socialagent deploy&lt;/code&gt; provisions the following resources (it runs &lt;code&gt;terraform apply&lt;/code&gt; under the hood).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime and AgentCore memory.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The webhook, notification, event, and scheduler Lambda functions, on Python 3.12, each with AWS X-Ray tracing and a dead-letter queue.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Amazon SNS ingestion topic and the event API.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon EventBridge Scheduler.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon S3 buckets for media and audit trails.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon CloudWatch dashboards and alarms.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent container image and the Lambda layer build in AWS CodeBuild during the apply. Docker isn’t required locally.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-you-need"&gt;What you need&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you deploy, confirm that you have the following.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS End User Messaging Social, with a WhatsApp Business Account (WABA) linked and a phone number registered. That account is the identity Meta issues to a business. For a step-by-step guide, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/social-messaging/latest/userguide/getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting started with AWS End User Messaging Social&lt;/a&gt;, which walks you through linking your WABA and registering a phone number.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with AgentCore runtime and AgentCore memory available in your AWS Region, and model access enabled. This walkthrough uses Claude Haiku 5.5 on Amazon Bedrock by default. You can access many more foundation models (FMs) through Bedrock. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model support by Region&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.11 or later locally.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Terraform 1.7 or later, uv, and the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI). The repository ships a starting-point AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policy for the deployer role. See the infrastructure README for the full list of actions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; This is sample code, for non-production usage. You should work with your security and legal teams to meet your organizational security, regulatory and compliance requirements before deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="ship-it"&gt;Ship it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start by cloning the repository and scaffolding a project.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/awslabs/social-messaging-agent-runtime-toolkit smart &amp;amp;&amp;amp; cd smart &amp;amp;&amp;amp; make install
uv run socialagent init my-bot --blueprint personal-assistant&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;--blueprint&lt;/code&gt; flag selects a project blueprint. Three blueprints ship, and &lt;code&gt;minimal&lt;/code&gt; is the default if you don’t pass an argument:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;minimal&lt;/code&gt;: a bare-bones echo agent with no tools, skills, or message templates. Start here to build from scratch.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;personal-assistant&lt;/code&gt;: a general-purpose assistant with conversation memory, per-user state, scheduling, skills, and rich/voice/image media tools, built on Strands. Start here for conversational, do-it-all agents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;appointment-scheduler&lt;/code&gt;: a booking agent that walks the user through a catalog of items and time slots, confirms the appointment, stores it as per-user state, and sends reminders. Start here for scheduling and booking flows.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You get a project with your &lt;code&gt;agent.py&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;socialagent.dev.yaml&lt;/code&gt; (the project’s deployment configuration), handler stubs for the webhook, notification, event, and scheduler paths, and a vendored copy of &lt;code&gt;socialagent_runtime/&lt;/code&gt;. The personal assistant and appointment-scheduler blueprints also bring tools, skills, and a &lt;code&gt;templates.yaml&lt;/code&gt; for your message templates. &lt;code&gt;minimal&lt;/code&gt; ships none of those, so you add them as required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next, iterate locally, with no infrastructure at all.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd projects/my-bot
uv run socialagent dev --entrypoint agent.py&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;socialagent dev&lt;/code&gt; command runs your real agent loop and real tools in a terminal read-eval-print loop (REPL), with AWS channel I/O stubbed. Memory becomes a local file-backed session, and state becomes a local JSON file shared across your dev sessions. Notifications print to the terminal, including buttons and list rows. One-shot schedules run immediately. Recurring ones are registered but do not fire. Tool calls appear inline. The reply arrives as one message, as on WhatsApp. The &lt;code&gt;/event&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;/schedule&lt;/code&gt; commands exercise the handler paths. No deployment is involved, so you can test a change as soon as you save it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two caveats apply. The model call is real, so a blueprint other than &lt;code&gt;minimal&lt;/code&gt; needs AWS credentials and Amazon Bedrock access. The REPL also doesn’t simulate IAM or Meta’s template review. Testing through a deployed stack is highly recommended before you consider something done.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can add capabilities as recipes. Each recipe is a plain Python function plus a markdown skill that teaches the agent when to use it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run socialagent recipe add voice-reply    # or rich-messages, generate-image&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Wiring the recipe to the agentic layer is the developer responsibility, which is what keeps recipes portable if you change agent frameworks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the behavior is right locally, deploy the stack.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run socialagent bootstrap   # once per account and stage
# set whatsapp.phone_number_id in socialagent.dev.yaml, then:
uv run socialagent deploy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One step Terraform cannot do for you. AWS End User Messaging Social needs to know where to deliver inbound messages. Point your WhatsApp Business Account at the Amazon SNS topic the stack created. That setting lives on the business account, not in the stack. Point the WABA’s event destination at the stack’s SNS topic using the admin console’s Bind action, or with &lt;code&gt;aws socialmessaging put-whatsapp-business-account-event-destinations&lt;/code&gt; from the CLI. Until it is linked, inbound messages go nowhere, and nothing errors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the project includes a &lt;code&gt;templates.yaml&lt;/code&gt;, the deploy step also syncs your message templates with Meta. If you need to sync templates independently, run &lt;code&gt;uv run socialagent templates sync&lt;/code&gt;, which reads the phone number ID and prefix from &lt;code&gt;socialagent.dev.yaml&lt;/code&gt; and the template definitions from &lt;code&gt;templates.yaml&lt;/code&gt;. A rejected template will fail the deploy, so fix the wording before retrying.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Meta sorts templates by purpose, and a transactional one has more permissive sending rules than a promotional one. Keep the wording plain. Emojis and marketing language are the usual reason a template gets reclassified as promotional. Running &lt;code&gt;templates sync --force&lt;/code&gt; re-registers every template, sending approved ones back through Meta review. A plain sync skips them. Leave approved templates alone unless you need to change their content.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now you’re ready to message your WhatsApp number.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="operate-it"&gt;Operate it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Shipping one bot is a project. Operating a fleet of them is the part nobody budgets for, so you also get an optional administration console with its own Terraform root. Deploy it once. Each SMART stack that sets &lt;code&gt;admin_registry_bucket&lt;/code&gt; registers itself to a shared Amazon S3 registry on deploy, and the administration console discovers those stacks from there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The console gives you fleet health at a glance, then per-bot depth. From a single stack view, you can do the following.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Read real conversations and reply by hand.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Inspect and cancel Amazon EventBridge schedules, and browse per-user state.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Restart an individual Lambda function or the runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review deployment history, recent errors, and Amazon CloudWatch logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Trigger test events.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Manage message templates with a WhatsApp-style rendering, and see a warning when Meta has reclassified one.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The console also owns the phone-number binding described earlier, so rebinding a number to a different stack is a UI action.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Sign-in runs through Amazon Cognito, with multi-factor authentication (MFA) required. Role-based permissions are then enforced on every API route. The console itself is served from Amazon CloudFront over a private Amazon S3 bucket.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The console runs on AWS services end to end. Every conversation stays within two providers: Meta and AWS. Model calls may be processed in other AWS Regions through Amazon Bedrock cross-Region inference. See &lt;code&gt;docs/data-flows.md&lt;/code&gt; in the repository for details on inference routing, media processing, and service opt-out options.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, tear down the stacks in the following order.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run socialagent destroy
make ui-destroy   # if you deployed the console&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Terraform will not remove three things. The media and audit S3 buckets block destroy while they hold objects, unless &lt;code&gt;enable_force_destroy&lt;/code&gt; is set. Templates registered with Meta persist. Your phone number registration is account-level and stays in place.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started"&gt;Get started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When building a WhatsApp AI product, the agent is where your differentiation lives. The channel integration is what eats your roadmap. SMART moves that boundary for you. Write a prompt, a few tools, and your business logic. In return you get webhook ingestion, media handling, conversation memory, scheduling, and template management. All backed by one-command infrastructure, a local development loop, and an administration console running in your own AWS account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To go further, take the following next steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Get the code from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/social-messaging-agent-runtime-toolkit" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SMART repository on GitHub&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set up your WhatsApp Business Account with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/social-messaging/latest/userguide/getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS End User Messaging Social&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Start with &lt;code&gt;socialagent init&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;socialagent dev&lt;/code&gt;, which need no AWS infrastructure.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Read the documentation in the repository for the areas this post summarized, including notifications, media handling, events and scheduling, per-user state, and message templates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For further research, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/what-is-bedrock-agentcore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We would love to see what you build with it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Reason over time-series data with OpenTSLM on Amazon SageMaker JumpStart</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reason-over-time-series-data-with-opentslm-on-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Martin Maritsch]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 18:06:44 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker JumpStart]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">55fd3d9060dc84442ff007934ddf9a781e246ca4</guid>

					<description>OpenTSLM, an open source family of time-series language models, is now available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart. See how Nexthink and Aionic Labs apply it to proactive IT support, then deploy OpenTSLM to a real-time endpoint and fine-tune it on your own data with TimeNet and a SageMaker training job.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Across industries, decision-making starts with understanding what is changing and why. Industrial organizations collect data that informs decisions across their operations. This data helps prevent production stoppages, diagnose vehicle faults, explain drops in data-center performance, and adjust pricing as supply and demand shift. However, interpreting that data still requires specialist time, and collecting more data does not mean teams can act on it. General-purpose language models like Claude or ChatGPT can help analyze data. However, processing long histories across hundreds of operational metrics as text or images can consume large numbers of tokens, which increases cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To help organizations act on this data, &lt;a href="https://www.aioniclabs.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Aionic Labs&lt;/a&gt; is developing temporal foundation models, including &lt;a href="https://github.com/OpenTSLM/OpenTSLM" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTSLM&lt;/a&gt;, an open source family of time-series language models (TSLMs). These models interpret operational measurements, from equipment sensor data to supply and demand, alongside context such as maintenance records, market news, and an engineer’s observations. In doing so, TSLMs help systems recognize emerging problems in recent measurements. Experts can then ask follow-up questions to understand what changed and assess how to respond. &lt;a href="https://docs.timenet.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TimeNet&lt;/a&gt;, the shared data infrastructure for time-series data, provides one format and API to find, build, and load the datasets used to train and evaluate these models. Together, they help organizations understand how their systems change and act before problems become costly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OpenTSLM is now available in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;, so you can deploy the model in your own AWS environment. This post explores how Nexthink and Aionic Labs are bringing this capability to proactive IT assistance, then shows how to deploy OpenTSLM and adapt it to your own data using TimeNet.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-1.png" alt="Diagram: numerical time series and a question with context go into OpenTSLM, where a time-series encoder feeds a language model. OpenTSLM returns natural-language output that describes patterns, answers questions, and suggests what to check." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1. Time-series language models combine numerical measurements with text context and questions. Potential applications span physical and digital systems, with adaptation and evaluation for each task.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="a-customer-example-proactive-it-support-at-nexthink"&gt;A customer example: Proactive IT support at Nexthink&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://nexthink.com/platform" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Nexthink&lt;/a&gt; and Aionic Labs are building toward a digital workplace where employees get help &lt;em&gt;before&lt;/em&gt; they need to ask for it. At the center is &lt;a href="https://nexthink.com/platform/spark" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Spark&lt;/a&gt;, Nexthink’s personal IT agent, enhanced with time-series language models that connect device telemetry and context to the problems employees experience. Spark turns that understanding into action: it checks the likely issue, guides the employee through an appropriate fix, and follows up on the result. Proactive assistance becomes part of keeping work moving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The workflow combines recent device time-series, such as CPU activity, memory use, available disk space, application stability, and connectivity, with timestamped events and device and software context. The TSLM receives the numerical measurements alongside this text context and interprets how the signals change over the observation window.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The TSLM provides Spark with three outputs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;State summary:&lt;/strong&gt; Explains the likely issue and the supporting signal patterns, connecting device behavior with the employee’s experience.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended action:&lt;/strong&gt; Specifies checks to confirm the issue and a practical action for Spark to offer the employee.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected outcome:&lt;/strong&gt; Describes the intended improvement and how Spark checks the result.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Device telemetry and context support investigation of a range of issues, including slow performance under high CPU or memory load, recurring application freezes or crashes, and files that fail to synchronize. Resource usage, application events, storage measurements, and connection activity provide different pieces of evidence. Combined with device configuration and software versions, they give the TSLM a broader basis for explaining the employee’s experience and directing Spark’s next checks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the illustrative low-disk example in Figure 2, the TSLM connects declining free space, a rising disk queue, and more frequent application hangs with likely employee symptoms, such as a slow computer or difficulty saving files. Spark checks how much space is left and whether temporary files can be removed using an IT-approved cleanup. If the checks confirm that a cleanup is appropriate, Spark shows the employee what it proposes to remove and asks for confirmation. After the employee confirms, Spark runs the cleanup, checks the space now available, and asks whether the problem has improved. If the issue persists, Spark offers to pass the findings to IT support.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TSLMs understand how systems change and help people act on that understanding. For Spark, this means recognizing emerging IT problems and guiding employees toward a solution. The same approach brings time series intelligence to manufacturing, energy, and connected products, turning measurements and context into explanations and intelligent decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-2.png" alt="Diagram of the low-disk-space scenario: the prompt, the laptop context, and the device history of falling free storage, a rising disk queue, and more application hangs go into OpenTSLM. OpenTSLM returns a state summary, a recommended cleanup that Spark runs after the employee confirms, and an expected outcome that Spark checks." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2. Illustrative proactive support workflow for low disk space at Nexthink. OpenTSLM generates a state summary, a recommended action, and an expected outcome from device history and context. Spark checks the device, runs an IT-approved cleanup after employee confirmation, and follows up on the result.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture in Figure 3 provides a reusable deployment pattern for time-series applications. SageMaker JumpStart supplies the model discovery and deployment path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt; is a machine learning (ML) model hub that accelerates your ML journey. With SageMaker JumpStart, you can evaluate, compare, and deploy pretrained foundation models (FMs), including the newly available OpenTSLM model. As part of the AWS AI offerings, SageMaker JumpStart provides customizable ML solutions which you can deploy to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/deploy-model.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI inference endpoints&lt;/a&gt; within your AWS infrastructure. It complements &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, which offers serverless API foundation model inference along with features like customizations, knowledge bases, agents, and guardrails.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Key technical benefits of SageMaker AI and SageMaker JumpStart include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Pre-built inference containers – &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/pre-built-containers-frameworks-deep-learning.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers&lt;/a&gt; with GPU acceleration, so you can serve the model without building your own image.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Automatic scaling endpoints – Managed infrastructure scales compute resources based on request volume and latency requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Inference API – SageMaker real-time inference endpoints with standardized API calls, SDK, and AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Integrated monitoring – Built-in metrics for throughput, latency, and resource utilization with an Amazon CloudWatch integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enterprise security – Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) deployment with IAM access control and network isolation options.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model in SageMaker JumpStart is &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2510.02410v3#S3.SS3" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTSLM-Flamingo&lt;/a&gt;. It combines the Gemma 3 4B language model with a time-series encoder and cross-attention layers that let the language model attend to the encoded series. The model has 5.24 billion parameters in total, stored in bfloat16, so its weights take about 10.5 GB of GPU memory.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An application prepares numerical time series and text context, invokes the SageMaker endpoint, and receives the model’s response. The application can present that response to an expert or use it as input to another workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This separation allows the application to control the observation window, select relevant signals, and validate the response before using it. It also makes the model independently adaptable: a new checkpoint can be evaluated against representative cases before the application switches to it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition, we show a tutorial that fine-tunes a pretrained OpenTSLM checkpoint from Hugging Face using ARFBench data loaded through TimeNet. The notebook submits a SageMaker training job that loads ARFBench through TimeNet and fine-tunes the model. Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) stores the resulting model artifact, which is then deployed to a separate SageMaker real-time inference endpoint for evaluation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-3.png" alt="Reference architecture: an application sends a request with a question and a CPU utilization series to an OpenTSLM real-time endpoint that was deployed from SageMaker JumpStart, and receives the generated answer." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3. A reusable architecture for deploying OpenTSLM through SageMaker JumpStart and invoking it from applications that combine time-series data with text context.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the deployment walkthrough, use an AWS account with an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/onboard-quick-start.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI domain&lt;/a&gt; and a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AI execution role&lt;/a&gt;. The role must be able to create SageMaker models and endpoints in a supported Region, for example a role with the &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMakerFullAccess&lt;/code&gt; managed policy. The quick setup for single users creates both. The managed policy grants broad permissions to get started. For production, use a policy that grants only the permissions you need, as described in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/security_iam_id-based-policy-examples.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI identity-based policy examples&lt;/a&gt;. OpenTSLM runs on a single GPU with at least 24 GB of memory and bfloat16 support, and we recommend 32 GB of host memory. The default instance type is &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; (one NVIDIA L4 GPU with 24 GB of memory, 32 GB of host memory). In SageMaker JumpStart, OpenTSLM also supports &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.8xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.8xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ml.g6e.xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;ml.g6e.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt;. In the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/servicequotas/home/services/sagemaker/quotas" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Service Quotas console&lt;/a&gt;, check your quota for “&lt;code&gt;ml.g6.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; for endpoint usage”, or for the instance type you choose. You need a quota of at least 1. If it’s lower, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/servicequotas/latest/userguide/request-quota-increase.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;request an increase&lt;/a&gt;. See the OpenTSLM JumpStart model card for the model access requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For fine-tuning, use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-updated-jl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JupyterLab in Amazon SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt; or a local Jupyter environment with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-chap-configure.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS credentials configured&lt;/a&gt; to run the accompanying notebook, which submits the training job. The notebook uses the SageMaker default S3 bucket and an execution role. Fine-tuning runs on &lt;code&gt;ml.g7e.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, so also check your quota for “&lt;code&gt;ml.g7e.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; for training job usage”. Training runs on the job’s managed GPU instance. The notebook itself does not need a GPU. Obtain access to the selected Hugging Face checkpoint where required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-opentslm-with-sagemaker-jumpstart"&gt;Deploy OpenTSLM with SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy OpenTSLM &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/jumpstart-foundation-models-use-studio-updated-deploy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;in SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt;, complete the following steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In SageMaker Studio, choose &lt;strong&gt;Models&lt;/strong&gt; in the navigation pane, then &lt;strong&gt;JumpStart models&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Search for “OpenTSLM” and choose &lt;strong&gt;OpenTSLM-Flamingo Gemma 3 4B&lt;/strong&gt;. The model page shows the model ID, the license, and the request and response format.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-4.png" alt="SageMaker Studio model page of OpenTSLM-Flamingo Gemma 3 4B with the Evaluate, Optimize, and Deploy buttons and the model information, including the JumpStart model ID, the gemma license, and open weights access." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The OpenTSLM-Flamingo Gemma 3 4B model page in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the &lt;strong&gt;Deploy model to endpoint&lt;/strong&gt; form, review the Gemma Terms of Use under &lt;strong&gt;License agreement&lt;/strong&gt; and select the checkbox to accept the end user license agreement (EULA). Keep the default instance type, &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, or choose another supported instance type. Then choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-5.png" alt="The Deploy model to endpoint form with the accepted license agreement, the default endpoint name, the instance type ml.g6.2xlarge, and real-time inference." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The deploy model to endpoint form with the default &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance type and real-time inference&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="5" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Wait until the endpoint status is &lt;strong&gt;InService&lt;/strong&gt;. In our tests, this took about 6 minutes. Note the endpoint name, which starts with &lt;code&gt;jumpstart-dft-&lt;/code&gt;. You need it to send requests and to clean up.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21774-6.png" alt="The endpoint summary of the deployed OpenTSLM endpoint in SageMaker Studio with the status In service." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: The deployed OpenTSLM endpoint with InService status in SageMaker Studio&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also create the deployment from Python with the SageMaker Python SDK v3. The following example uses ModelBuilder. The model is gated: setting &lt;code&gt;accept_eula=True&lt;/code&gt; accepts its license, the Gemma Terms of Use. Review the terms before you deploy. The SDK prints a link to them when it builds the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.core.helper.session_helper import get_execution_role
from sagemaker.core.jumpstart.configs import JumpStartConfig
from sagemaker.serve.model_builder import ModelBuilder
from sagemaker.train.configs import Compute

MODEL_ID = "pytorch-reasoning-opentslm-flamingo-gemma3-4b"
INSTANCE_TYPE = "ml.g6.2xlarge"  # 1 x NVIDIA L4 (24 GB); ml.g5.2xlarge and ml.g6e.2xlarge also work
ROLE_ARN = get_execution_role()  # in SageMaker Studio; elsewhere, set your execution role ARN

builder = ModelBuilder.from_jumpstart_config(
    jumpstart_config=JumpStartConfig(model_id=MODEL_ID, accept_eula=True),
    compute=Compute(instance_type=INSTANCE_TYPE),
    role_arn=ROLE_ARN,
)
builder.build(model_name="opentslm-blog-demo")
endpoint = builder.deploy(endpoint_name="opentslm-blog-demo")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="ask-a-question-about-a-time-series"&gt;Ask a question about a time series&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The endpoint accepts a JSON request with one or more instances. Each instance holds a question, one or more numerical series, and optional context lines. Each series has its values, a name, and a short description of the signal, such as its unit and sampling interval. A series can hold up to 32,768 values. The endpoint scales each series to a mean of 0 and a standard deviation of 1 and passes the original mean and standard deviation to the model as text, so the model receives both the shape and the scale of the signal. Use &lt;code&gt;None&lt;/code&gt; for a missing value. The model masks it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Send the request with the SageMaker Runtime &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_runtime_InvokeEndpoint.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;InvokeEndpoint&lt;/a&gt; API. Set ENDPOINT_NAME to match your deployment. The calling identity needs the &lt;code&gt;sagemaker:InvokeEndpoint&lt;/code&gt; permission. The following synthetic CPU example shows a request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json

import boto3

ENDPOINT_NAME = "opentslm-blog-demo"
runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-runtime")

# One hour of CPU utilization, one sample per minute, with a five-minute spike.
cpu = [21, 20, 22, 19, 21, 20, 23, 21, 20, 19, 22, 21, 20, 21, 19, 22, 20, 21, 23, 20,
       21, 19, 20, 22, 21, 20, 19, 21, 78, 85, 82, 80, 79, 22, 20, 21, 19, 20, 22, 21,
       20, 21, 23, 19, 20, 21, 22, 20, 19, 21, 20, 22, 21, 20, 19, 21, 22, 20, 21, 20]

request = {
    "instances": [{
        "question": "Summarize the pattern and suggest what to check.",
        "series": [{
            "values": cpu,  # use None for a missing value
            "name": "CPU utilization (%)",
            "text": "CPU utilization (%), 1 sample/minute:",
        }],
        "context": ["The series covers 10:00 to 10:59 on a web server."],  # optional
    }],
    "parameters": {"max_new_tokens": 128},
}

response = runtime.invoke_endpoint(
    EndpointName=ENDPOINT_NAME,
    ContentType="application/json",
    Body=json.dumps(request),
)
print(json.loads(response["Body"].read())["generated_text"][0])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A request can hold up to four instances, each with 1 to 32 series, and the response contains one answer per instance in &lt;code&gt;generated_text&lt;/code&gt;. &lt;code&gt;max_new_tokens&lt;/code&gt; accepts values from 1 to 512. The model uses greedy decoding, so the same request typically returns the same answer. A real-time invocation must complete within 60 seconds, and the request body can be up to 6 MB. On &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, the model generates about 20 tokens per second. The endpoint processes the instances of a request in sequence, so response time grows with the number of instances and the length of the answers: an answer of 512 tokens takes about 30 seconds. To stay within 60 seconds, send several instances in one request only with short answers, for example with &lt;code&gt;max_new_tokens&lt;/code&gt; of 128. The 6 MB limit also bounds how much series data a request can carry. For long-running requests, you can use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/async-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;asynchronous inference&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can send the same request with the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI). Save the request as JSON, for example with &lt;code&gt;json.dump(request, open("request.json", "w"))&lt;/code&gt;, then run:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker-runtime invoke-endpoint \
    --endpoint-name opentslm-blog-demo \
    --content-type application/json \
    --body fileb://request.json \
    response.json
cat response.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="choose-an-input-window-that-preserves-relevant-events"&gt;Choose an input window that preserves relevant events&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2510.02410v3#S3.SS3" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTSLM-Flamingo&lt;/a&gt; compresses the encoded time series into a fixed-size set of representations before the language model uses it. This bounds the representation passed to the language model, but it does not make the complete system unlimited in memory or guarantee that a brief event remains easy to identify in a very long recording.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Long-context research makes this distinction concrete. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2602.14200v6" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TS-Haystack&lt;/a&gt; evaluates event retrieval across recordings of different lengths and finds that the evaluated TSLM variants often lose accuracy as the history grows, especially when locating event start and end times. These results motivate testing the chosen checkpoint on the windows and questions used by your application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose a window that contains the event and enough surrounding context to interpret it. For long recordings, compare overlapping windows. Preserve sampling resolution for brief events and align related channels in time. Include units, sampling interval, and the observation period in the text context. Validate both normal and abnormal examples at the intended lengths. The endpoint’s accepted payload size and the model’s useful analysis window are separate constraints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="fine-tune-opentslm-on-arfbench-with-sagemaker"&gt;Fine-tune OpenTSLM on ARFBench with SageMaker&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/generative-ai-on-amazon-sagemaker/tree/main/examples/opentslm-finetune-arfbench" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying fine-tuning notebook&lt;/a&gt; for the complete example, from preparing ARFBench data with TimeNet to launching a SageMaker training job and deploying the adapted model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="when-should-you-fine-tune"&gt;When should you fine-tune?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start by testing the pretrained OpenTSLM model on representative examples from your application, and assess how well it answers your questions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use the pretrained model&lt;/strong&gt; when its answers are correct and its explanations match the signals.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Improve the input&lt;/strong&gt; when important information is missing, such as measurement units, sampling rate, system context, or the relevant time window.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fine-tune the model&lt;/strong&gt; when it consistently misses patterns specific to your domain or struggles with your task, even with sufficient context. Use representative signals paired with reviewed answers.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.timenet.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TimeNet&lt;/a&gt; provides a consistent way to load the data for training and evaluation. After fine-tuning, compare the adapted model with the original on examples that were not used for training.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prepare-fine-tuning-data-with-timenet"&gt;Prepare fine-tuning data with TimeNet&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.datadoghq.com/blog/ai/introducing-arfbench/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ARFBench&lt;/a&gt; is a public benchmark of multiple-choice questions about anomalies in Datadog’s software-incident telemetry. We use ARFBench because its multiple-choice answers can be scored automatically, which makes the effect of fine-tuning measurable. The same workflow applies to tasks with open-ended answers, such as the summaries and recommended actions in the Nexthink example. TimeNet serves ARFBench as a versioned dataset: each question is stored with its answer options, its correct answer, and the metric signals. The training job loads ARFBench through TimeNet and the model checkpoint from Hugging Face.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="submit-a-sagemaker-training-job-and-deploy-the-fine-tuned-model"&gt;Submit a SageMaker training job and deploy the fine-tuned model&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A SageMaker training job runs the OpenTSLM training script on a GPU instance. The job fine-tunes the time-series encoder, the perceiver resampler, the cross-attention layers, and the language model’s token embeddings, and keeps the rest of the Gemma 3 language model frozen. The job saves the fine-tuned model to Amazon S3 in the layout of the Hugging Face model repository and writes the test accuracy before and after training to a &lt;code&gt;metrics.json&lt;/code&gt; file. If your SageMaker Studio domain has a default MLflow App, the job also logs the training and validation loss and the test accuracy to it, so you can compare runs in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/mlflow-launch-ui.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MLflow UI&lt;/a&gt;. In our run on one &lt;code&gt;ml.g7e.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance, accuracy on 135 held-out ARFBench questions rose from 5.0% to 45.9%. That is well above the 24.5% that random choice reaches on the full benchmark according to the &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21199" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ARFBench paper&lt;/a&gt;. Before fine-tuning, the model left most answers empty. Afterwards, it answered every question with one of the given options. ARFBench serves here as an example application: it shows how to adapt the model to a new task and answer format with a public dataset. The result is not a comparison with other models or an official ARFBench leaderboard result.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following excerpt from the accompanying notebook starts the training job with &lt;code&gt;ModelTrainer&lt;/code&gt;. &lt;code&gt;scripts/train.py&lt;/code&gt; loads ARFBench, evaluates and fine-tunes the model, and saves it to &lt;code&gt;FINETUNED_PREFIX&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;trainer = ModelTrainer(
    training_image=TRAINING_IMAGE,  # the SageMaker PyTorch 2.9 training image
    source_code=SourceCode(
        source_dir="scripts", entry_script="train.py", requirements="requirements.txt"
    ),
    compute=Compute(instance_type="ml.g7e.2xlarge", volume_size_in_gb=100),
    stopping_condition=StoppingCondition(max_runtime_in_seconds=24 * 60 * 60),
    hyperparameters={"base-model": BASE_MODEL, "output": FINETUNED_PREFIX, "epochs": 3},
    role=ROLE_ARN,
)
trainer.train()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy the fine-tuned model, create a new real-time endpoint on &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, or another instance type that the JumpStart model supports, with the same container image and environment variables as the JumpStart model, and the inference script that the accompanying notebook provides. Point its model data to the S3 location of the fine-tuned model files. The request format stays the same, so you can send it the requests from the previous section. The accompanying notebook contains the code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="adapt-the-workflow-to-your-domain"&gt;Adapt the workflow to your domain&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same workflow can be adapted to other time-series tasks by pairing signal windows with relevant questions and reviewed answers. Potential examples include interpreting equipment behavior with maintenance context, explaining energy-consumption patterns with operating conditions, and describing activity from wearable measurements. The data, supervision, and evaluation should reflect the domain and the decision the application is intended to support.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Preserve the timing and meaning of the measurements during preparation, and separate training and evaluation data in a way that reflects deployment. For a predictive task, only information available at prediction time belongs in the input. Domain experts should assess whether an answer is grounded in the signals and useful for the intended workflow. For free-text answers, compare them with reviewed reference answers, for example through expert ratings or LLM-as-a-judge, where a language model scores each answer against a rubric.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deployed endpoints continue to incur charges until you delete them. When you finish, delete the endpoint, its endpoint configuration, and its model. If you deployed from SageMaker Studio, run the following commands instead of choosing &lt;strong&gt;Delete&lt;/strong&gt; on the endpoint page, which deletes only the endpoint. The commands look up the configuration and model names from the endpoint name and delete all three. Set &lt;code&gt;ENDPOINT_NAME&lt;/code&gt; to the name of your endpoint, for example the default name that starts with &lt;code&gt;jumpstart-dft-&lt;/code&gt; for a Studio deployment:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;ENDPOINT_NAME=opentslm-blog-demo
CONFIG_NAME=$(aws sagemaker describe-endpoint --endpoint-name "$ENDPOINT_NAME" \
    --query EndpointConfigName --output text)
MODEL_NAME=$(aws sagemaker describe-endpoint-config --endpoint-config-name "$CONFIG_NAME" \
    --query 'ProductionVariants[0].ModelName' --output text)

aws sagemaker delete-endpoint --endpoint-name "$ENDPOINT_NAME"
aws sagemaker delete-endpoint-config --endpoint-config-name "$CONFIG_NAME"
aws sagemaker delete-model --model-name "$MODEL_NAME"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Repeat these commands with the name of the fine-tuned endpoint. Stop the notebook application and remove tutorial-specific S3 data and artifacts that you do not intend to retain. The training job stops when it completes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Time-series language models connect numerical measurements with the questions, context, and expertise people use to make decisions. Across manufacturing, energy, wearables, and digital infrastructure, they give people and applications a common way to interpret how systems change. The broader opportunity is to connect operational history with language, making past observations more useful in current decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OpenTSLM is now available in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;, so you can deploy it to a real-time inference endpoint in your own AWS environment through SageMaker Studio or the SageMaker SDK. TimeNet and SageMaker training jobs provide a path to adapt the model to your domain’s signals and questions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;, follow the examples in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/generative-ai-on-amazon-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Samples repository&lt;/a&gt;, and consult the &lt;a href="https://github.com/OpenTSLM/OpenTSLM" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTSLM repository&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.timenet.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TimeNet documentation&lt;/a&gt;. Start with the public ARFBench example, evaluate it on representative cases, then bring the signals, questions, and expertise from your own domain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
