 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Responsible AI for agentic AI: A phased roadmap for platform and application teams</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/responsible-ai-for-agentic-ai-a-phased-roadmap-for-platform-and-application-teams/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mia Chang]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 16:56:31 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Generative AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">880ddd5d6ca4ac6c3010cbbe57b7883439fc2199</guid>

					<description>Enterprises adopting agentic AI at scale need platforms that embed responsible AI from the start. This post gives platform and application teams a three-phase roadmap (governance foundation, pilot deployment, and evaluation and hardening) that maps the AWS Responsible AI Lens pillars to each phase, with guidance on when to revisit each one.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Companies build agentic AI platforms to govern the applications, tools, skills, models, and storage that agents use. The nondeterministic nature of generative AI models introduces a complexity that previous software development practices didn’t anticipate. Consider a real estate app that generates property listings from retrieved data: does it accurately reflect the property, or hallucinate features that don’t exist? Or a customer service agent grounded on internal knowledge bases: can you verify that it doesn’t surface confidential information?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Until recently, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/responsible-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;responsible AI&lt;/a&gt; (RAI) was model-centric, scoped to the machine learning development lifecycle: training, testing, and inference. Models didn’t reason, use tools, or take actions. When the conversation extended to model-powered systems, it covered statistically bounded situations you could test against known inputs. Generative AI powered applications and agents changed this. The systems now reason, act, compose tools dynamically, and interact over multiple steps, expanding both the scope and the depth of what responsible AI must cover.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations are turning Responsible AI, which includes the pillars of &lt;strong&gt;security and privacy, veracity and robustness, fairness, safety, controllability, explainability, transparency, and governance&lt;/strong&gt; into a growth strategy by addressing its expanding scope early. When enterprises adopt agentic AI at scale, the positive and negative impacts come hand in hand. As Wade and Wirtz observe in the &lt;a href="https://hbr.org/2026/07/responsible-ai-is-becoming-a-growth-strategy" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Harvard Business Review&lt;/a&gt;, trust is becoming a competitive differentiator. The same capabilities that personalize at scale can also surveil and discriminate, and organizations that govern them earn stakeholder confidence. Robinhood, for example, built a generative AI financial crimes agent on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; with strict data governance. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/solutions/case-studies/robinhood-case-study/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;They achieved a 20% efficiency gain&lt;/a&gt; while maintaining regulatory compliance. Structuring these practices across platform maturity is the next step. In this post, we refer to the team that manages the platform as the Platform team, and to the teams that build on it as Application teams. Application teams, sometimes called Workstream teams, build and own agents on the platform.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post provides a condensed, actionable roadmap for the Platform team and Application teams building on agentic AI. It’s designed to be read together: the Platform team sets up the guardrails. Application teams build within them. Both need a shared language for where they are and what &lt;em&gt;good&lt;/em&gt; looks like. We provide a &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-agent-skills-for-builders/tree/main/skills/agentic-responsible-ai-assessment" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Responsible AI Assessment Skill&lt;/a&gt;, that maps the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/responsible-ai-lens/responsible-ai-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Responsible AI Lens&lt;/a&gt; pillars to a three-phase maturity model: &lt;strong&gt;governance foundations, pilot deployment, and evaluation and hardening&lt;/strong&gt;. For each phase, it shows which responsible AI pillars are critical and when to revisit them. An accompanying scorecard lets both application teams and the platform team assess where they are today and iterate as their applications mature.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; This post covers the key questions to ask at each phase. It leaves out step-by-step implementation details. This post also doesn’t provide legal or compliance advice. Always consult your legal team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge"&gt;The challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In conversations with customers building agentic AI platforms, four challenges surface consistently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unpredictable runtime behavior&lt;/strong&gt;: nondeterministic outputs, goal drift, and cascading failures where one agent error propagates across services. A financial services company in Germany found that multi-agent orchestration amplified a single misclassification into policy violations across three downstream services.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;b&gt;Data exposure and authority boundaries&lt;/b&gt;: Scope every agent to its intended tenants, data, systems, and actions. Isolate session context so each session starts with data residency at the access layer, and grant privileged access through least-privilege, task-bound permissions that agents can’t modify themselves. Agents then act only in the systems and with the authority they were explicitly given.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Regulatory velocity&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;a href="https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/regulatory-framework-ai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EU AI Act&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.iso.org/standard/42001" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ISO 42001&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.nist.gov/itl/ai-risk-management-framework" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NIST AI RMF&lt;/a&gt;, and upcoming legislation are creating compliance requirements faster than governance can adapt. Proving compliance for nondeterministic systems requires continuous evaluation, which should be monitored and revisited with a predefined cadence (for example, daily, weekly, or monthly).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Team collaboration&lt;/strong&gt;: organizations lack a shared framework defining who owns what across the agent lifecycle. A media company in the UK flagged that segregation of duty between developer and deployment teams is undefined for agent workloads. The Platform team builds guardrails that application teams work around, and application teams defer responsibility they assume the platform handles.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-21131-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-21131-1.png" alt="Four responsible AI challenges for agentic platforms: unpredictable runtime behavior, data boundaries, regulatory velocity, and team collaboration" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Agentic AI platforms introduce novel challenges regarding responsible AI.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-a-three-phase-responsible-ai-maturity-framework"&gt;Solution: A three-phase responsible AI maturity framework&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we define an agentic AI platform as described in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/agentops-operationalize-agentic-ai-at-scale-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentOps: Operationalize agentic AI at scale with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, a system for building, deploying, and operating AI agents securely at scale, covering governance, build and operations, evaluation, and observability. Your responsible AI posture should mature progressively alongside this platform. We break the journey into three phases and present prioritized responsible AI pillars across a customizable 12-week implementation timeline, allowing the platform team to establish a solid responsible AI foundation while their first use cases take shape.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/21131-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/21131-1.png" alt="Responsible AI maturity framework mapping pillars across three phases: governance foundation, pilot deployment, and evaluation and hardening" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Overview of the proposed responsible AI maturity framework for agentic AI platforms. Teams integrate responsible AI over three phases.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="phase-1-governance-foundation"&gt;Phase 1: Governance foundation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Focus pillars:&lt;/strong&gt; Governance, security and privacy&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this phase, assess project fit, risks, and ethics, secure legal and compliance approvals, and define the trust model for AI agents. The application teams classify risks for each planned use case using a risk rubric. The Platform team focuses on establishing the core landing zone infrastructure, for example, setting up the overarching monitoring and access control, and governing newly onboarded agents and use cases from the start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Critical tasks to consider in this phase include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="security-and-privacy"&gt;Security and privacy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Evaluate access to data and systems needed by each agent and assess potential impact. This lays the foundation for the knowledge grounding strategy.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Implement foundational data security and data exfiltration protection with data classification, data access control, logging, data integrity, and data access monitoring.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Build a map of the data that is shared between agents like organizational knowledge bases and the subjective data like context graphs which individual agents might need. Then establish access boundaries and assign read/write permissions accordingly.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="governance"&gt;Governance&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/learn-how-to-assess-risk-of-ai-systems/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Define a risk classification rubric&lt;/a&gt; aligned to your regulatory obligations (&lt;a href="https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/regulatory-framework-ai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EU AI Act&lt;/a&gt; risk tiers, financial services industry requirements, and so on). Assign Low / Medium / High / Unacceptable risk levels to each planned use case and document the control requirements each risk level triggers. Gather relevant documentation and traceability requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Before onboarding agents, the underlying models need to go through a vetting procedure. The procedure covers a series of evaluations with safety and reliability criteria. Catalog approved models based on capability, latency, and cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Implement a process to centrally register and govern products like agents, shared tools and skills deployed in your organization for centralized visibility and consumption. Every product gets registered with owner, risk tier, and regulatory scope. If a resource (agent, tool, skill) isn’t in the registry, it can’t be discovered and managed by the organization.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="phase-2-pilot-deployment"&gt;Phase 2: Pilot deployment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Focus pillars:&lt;/strong&gt; Safety, veracity and robustness, controllability, security and privacy&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploy your first agent that serves measurable business value. With the template and developer guidance provided from the Platform team, application teams implement orchestration patterns, memory, guardrail baselines, knowledge grounding, and tool use patterns. Risks identified in the foundation phase must now be addressed through a set of concrete controls in a pilot implementation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this phase, application teams consider the following concerns:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="safety"&gt;Safety&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Guardrails. Platform team establishes foundational personally identifiable information (PII) redaction filters for model I/O and tool calls. Provide a set of guardrails rules ready to be applied. Application teams then set content moderation thresholds explicitly based on the use case the agent serves. Configure human escalation for low-confidence responses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Policies. Define which agent can access what tool and how. Augment with fallback scenarios in case of policy violations.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="veracity-and-robustness"&gt;Veracity and robustness&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Knowledge grounding with checks: Even with the right context, models can still hallucinate or misrepresent the provided sources. Therefore, configure contextual grounding checks to verify every response is correctly grounded in relevant data. For regulated use cases, activate automated reasoning for verifiable outputs. Implement a process to curate and update your knowledge sources. Define how out-of-domain requests are classified and handled.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="controllability"&gt;Controllability&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Implement a process for &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsec04-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Human-in-the-loop approval for high-risk or on-behalf-of actions&lt;/a&gt; as circuit breakers. Define criteria for when an agent must defer to a human approver/reviewer (for example, confidence below threshold, safety guardrail triggered, or a high-stakes decision). Log escalation events for later audit and analysis.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Provide permission audits to the end-user to grant/revoke access provided by human users or agents that granted permissions to sub-agents to gain transparent control.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Activate end-to-end observability with session-level, trace-level, and span-level telemetry. Emit structured traces for agent decisions, guardrail invocations, and tool calls. Implement distributed tracing across multi-agent and multi-tool workflows to support full reconstruction of agent reasoning paths. Add dashboards for 360-degree observability and cost-control.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="security-and-privacy-1"&gt;Security and privacy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Establish &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsec02-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;least-privilege access controls&lt;/a&gt;. Define policy-based authorization rules for tool-level access using declarative policy languages. Apply granular access policies for agent actions and per knowledge source.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="phase-3-evaluation-and-hardening"&gt;Phase 3: Evaluation and hardening&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Focus pillars:&lt;/strong&gt; Fairness, explainability, transparency, security and privacy&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You have seen your first agent deliver business value. But how can you demonstrate it to a regulator, an auditor, or a board? How do you detect and help prevent unauthorized events on the system? In the third phase, evaluation stress-tests the fairness thresholds, and bias benchmarking. Hardening turns those findings into fixes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This phase includes the following workstreams:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="fairness"&gt;Fairness&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model-level fairness testing: Check model cards for fairness metrics and restrict model access to a list of pre-approved models tested for common biases. In case there is no metric provided, test bias and alignment yourself using established benchmark sets such as Compositional Evaluation Benchmark (&lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.02408" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CEB&lt;/a&gt;) or Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (&lt;a href="https://crfm.stanford.edu/helm/safety/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HELM&lt;/a&gt;). Monitor whether outputs and routing treat demographic groups equitably.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agent-level fairness testing: Evaluate whether end-to-end agent routing, including tool selection, and resource allocation treats user groups equitably.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Fairness regression monitoring: Set up automated pipelines to detect fairness drift over time as models are updated or new data sources are added.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="transparency"&gt;Transparency&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enforceable transparency for machine generated content: Attach machine-readable provenance labels to every reusable output, so downstream consumers can verify origin. Enforce mandatory AI-presence disclosure in user-facing interactions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Decision audit trails: Each agent action is logged with enough detail to reconstruct the reasoning path for a given outcome. For instance, what did the agent access, when and with which identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="explainability"&gt;Explainability&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Compile an audit report for a fictional audit or appeal: Make sure you can quickly assemble the relevant data and understand how a decision was made.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;User-facing explanations: Provide end-users with on-demand, plain-language summaries of why an agent took a specific action or made a recommendation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="security-and-privacy-2"&gt;Security and privacy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Continuous evaluation: Set up evaluation pipelines and evaluate before and after deployment. Document the results. Implement alerting mechanisms to be notified when metrics fall below a predefined quality threshold.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Conduct penetration testing and red-team evaluations targeting agent-specific threat models: prompt injection, tenant isolation under concurrent access, token exchange with misconfigured profiles, and long-running async agent tasks. Define a Security Incident Response runbook specific to AI agent safety events. Configure automated alerting for safety-critical events.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Fine-tune release criteria (evaluation scores, security scan results, fairness metrics) iteratively based on production feedback. Build deployment gates into your continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipeline. Nothing reaches production without meeting the release criteria for its risk tier.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="when-to-revisit"&gt;When to revisit&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Responsible AI isn’t a one-time implementation. As the platform evolves, each dimension must be periodically reassessed. The following table gives some examples to define both standing review cadences and event-driven triggers for re-evaluation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Standing Cadence&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Compliance assessment for in-scope agents: Documentation refresh, re-run evidence collection, human oversight logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Review risk classification, incident post-mortems.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Review onboarding and decommissioning procedures.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Continuous evaluation with drift detection autonomy, risk scoring and alerting.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Track false positive and false negative rates of Guardrails and adjust when needed.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Refresh knowledge bases continuously, remove outdated documents.&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Triggers&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;New use case is onboarded.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;New model added or model updates.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;New tools added.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Onboarding new customer segment.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;New risk identified, for example, a new security threat discovered.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Agent updated, for example, a new system prompt.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Compliance or regulatory change.&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Agent behaviors change.&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-use-case-and-implementation"&gt;Example use case and implementation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider the following case: A bank builds a multi-agent loan processing platform. The platform features an intake agent, risk scoring agent, compliance agent, and customer communication agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 1 (Governance foundation)&lt;/strong&gt;: The platform team gathers legal requirements for their use case, for example those stemming from the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/building-trust-in-ai-the-aws-approach-to-the-eu-ai-act/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EU AI Act&lt;/a&gt; and financial regulation, and registers agents at High-risk tier (financial decisions affecting individuals) using the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/the-future-of-managing-agents-at-scale-aws-agent-registry-now-in-preview/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt;. They use a secure &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/controltower/latest/userguide/aws-multi-account-landing-zone.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;landing zone built with AWS Control Tower&lt;/a&gt; with foundational logging and access control active and configure &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-sessions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;tenant isolation&lt;/a&gt; to help prevent cross-customer leakage. The team &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/incorporating-responsible-ai-into-generative-ai-project-prioritization/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documents residual risk and scope for each agent&lt;/a&gt;: for example, if the intake agent produces unexpected data, the risk scoring agent processes its output without independent verification.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 2 (Pilot deployment: Validation and rollout)&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsec08-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Risk scoring&lt;/a&gt; uses &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/knowledge-base.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt; grounded in regulatory lending documentation. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-verifiable-explainability-into-financial-services-workflows-with-automated-reasoning-checks-for-amazon-bedrock-guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automated Reasoning checks&lt;/a&gt; verify denial decisions conform to documented criteria (conformance, not causal explanation). &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/detect-and-redact-personally-identifiable-information-using-amazon-bedrock-data-automation-and-guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails redact PII&lt;/a&gt; data before customer communication. To address the multi-agent trust gap: the scoring agent confirms key applicant data from source systems rather than solely relying on the intake agent’s extraction. Each agent is bounded to required tools through AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions, and the team uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/why-policy-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-chose-cedar-for-securing-agentic-workflows/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policies in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; to further restrict the risk scoring agent to read-only access on the customer segment the agent is approved for. A case where the Automated Reasoning check returns a result other than VALID (for example, SATISFIABLE or TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS), or where the applicant’s risk score falls within the marginal band (for example, ±5 points of the threshold), is automatically escalated to a human credit specialist (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/human-in-the-loop-constructs-for-agentic-workflows-in-healthcare-and-life-sciences/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;human-in-the-loop review&lt;/a&gt;) before the decision is communicated. Escalation events are logged with the triggering condition for audit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 3 (Evaluation and hardening: Continuous monitoring)&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-reliable-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-evaluations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, run on every pipeline push. CI/CD blocks deployment if an evaluation result is below releasing criterion or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/responsible-ai-lens/rairc03-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;per-subgroup fairness metrics&lt;/a&gt; drifts beyond threshold. Red-team exercises (independent security team) test realistic security threats on the intake agent to verify that it can’t influence scoring. Human review of denial decisions, moving to after-the-fact review through an appeals process after 2,000 aligned decisions over 60 days. AI usage in the decision process is disclosed transparently to customers. Every automated decision is traced end-to-end using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-trustworthy-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-observability/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. It captures &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-telemetry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTelemetry spans&lt;/a&gt; that can be automatically aggregated into a single distributed trace ready for auditing and appeals.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-to-measure-maturity"&gt;How to measure maturity&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Frameworks serve as aspirations, while measurements provide accountability. Questions like “Can you list the risk tier for a use case?” will necessarily arise when the platform is operational from stakeholders like CISOs or auditors. To leave you prepared, we provide a repository with a set of questions that you can use to gauge your current posture and identify improvement areas before your system goes live.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We also package the questionnaire as a portable &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-agent-skills-for-builders/tree/main/skills/agentic-responsible-ai-assessment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;skill&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt; for AI Agents like &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, and AI coding assistants like &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Kiro&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/solutions/claude-code-with-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Claude Code&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, and &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/openai-models-and-codex-on-amazon-bedrock-are-now-generally-available/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Codex&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt;. The AI walks you through each phase and assesses your responsible AI posture along the dimensions on a scale from 1–5. Scores below 4 indicate substantial risks that should be remediated. Scores of 4 or higher indicate residual risk that might be acceptable when acknowledged on a case-by-case basis. The AI also provides actionable recommendations for improving your Responsible AI posture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-21131-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-21131-3.png" alt="Portable AI skill scoring a team’s responsible AI posture across the eight pillars" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: A portable AI skill that helps teams assess their Responsible AI posture through a set of questions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Responsible AI (RAI) is a continuous, evolving effort. We recommend organizations implement RAI pillars through the AgentOps journey. These best practices cover governance policies, evaluation gates, and production telemetry into agentic AI platforms. This creates the feedback loop that keeps fairness, safety, and transparency continuously measured and improved.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This framework is a starting point, not an exhaustive compliance checklist. RAI practices vary by industry, use case, and requirements. Organizations should consult their own legal, compliance, and ethics teams to tailor these recommendations to their specific regulatory obligations and risk profiles.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a comprehensive treatment of responsible AI across workload types, refer to the AWS &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/responsible-ai-lens/responsible-ai-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Well-Architected Responsible AI Lens&lt;/a&gt;, which provides detailed best practices, design principles, and evaluation criteria for each of the eight pillars discussed in this post. For industry-specific guidance, consult the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/financial-services-industry-lens/financial-services-industry-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Financial Services Industry Lens&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/healthcare-industry-lens/healthcare-industry-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Healthcare Industry Lens&lt;/a&gt;, which address sector-specific regulatory and ethical requirements. Get started today by installing the RAI assessment skill from this &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-agent-skills-for-builders/tree/main/skills/agentic-responsible-ai-assessment" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;repository&lt;/a&gt; and evaluating your organization’s RAI posture with your preferred AI agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How ArisGlobal scaled generative AI inference on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-arisglobal-scaled-generative-ai-inference-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Shubham Gupta]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 15:57:09 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">0a44cff15a84618242302af42dadcc4ef349495d</guid>

					<description>ArisGlobal runs NavaX, its life-sciences AI engine, on Amazon Bedrock. By caching the shared prompt prefix instead of each per-case document, ArisGlobal cut effective input cost by about 50% and overall monthly inference spend by about 40%, while onboarding more tenants without proportional cost growth.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;ArisGlobal, a provider of life sciences technology spanning pharmacovigilance, regulatory, medical affairs, clinical, and quality functions, developed NavaX, an AI engine purpose-built for life sciences on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, to automate adverse-event case processing. ArisGlobal chose Amazon Bedrock for managed access to foundation models, EU geographic cross-Region inference, and managed prompt caching. As enterprise customers added distinct compliance requirements, the shared prompt prefix grew by about 150% over 6 months, while per-case inference costs increased by more than 200%.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ArisGlobal first cached each source document because it was the largest part of a request. That left the growing shared prompt prefix outside the cache. By reversing the order and caching the shared prefix instead, ArisGlobal reduced effective input cost and supported additional tenants without proportional cost growth.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="who-is-arisglobal"&gt;Who is ArisGlobal?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ArisGlobal is the creator of LifeSphere, a unified SaaS platform for pharmacovigilance, regulatory affairs, quality, and medical information. LifeSphere serves pharmaceutical companies globally, including multiple Top 20 pharma organizations by revenue (source: &lt;a href="https://www.arisglobal.com/media/press-release/arisglobal-reports-strong-q1-2026-momentum-with-accelerating-enterprise-ai-transformation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ArisGlobal press release&lt;/a&gt;). The entire system runs on AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.arisglobal.com/navax/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LifeSphere NavaX&lt;/a&gt; is ArisGlobal’s AI engine that transforms drug safety from a system of record into a system of strategic advantage. ArisGlobal productionized their first generative AI solution on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/claude/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock with Anthropic’s Claude&lt;/a&gt; in March 2025. According to ArisGlobal’s internal production telemetry, they have processed over 1 million real transactions in regulated pharmaceutical environments, handling billions of tokens across multiple enterprise tenants. Model availability varies by Region. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model support by AWS Region&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-navax-processes-an-adverse-event-case"&gt;How NavaX processes an adverse event case&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a pharmaceutical company receives an adverse-event report, typically a multipage PDF describing a patient’s reaction to a medication, NavaX’s Dynamic Data Extraction (DDE) agent extracts around 130 fields. These fields include patient demographics, suspect drugs, reporter information, event timelines, seriousness criteria, and regulatory classifications. Because this content includes protected health information, ArisGlobal applies responsible-AI controls as a standard part of its production deployment. It draws on capabilities such as &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; to filter content and detect or mask sensitive information. Extracted fields are also validated against the schema and grounded in the source document.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This extraction happens across multiple sequential requests to the model. Each request focuses on a different section of the case form, and each carries:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;System instructions — model behavior rules, output format specifications, and regulatory compliance constraints.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Customer-specific rules — per-tenant extraction logic, field mappings, and business rules that differ by pharmaceutical company.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Extraction schema — around 130 target fields with types, validation rules, and few-shot examples.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The source document — the adverse event PDF (often 10–200 pages).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two inputs repeat at different scopes. The shared prompt prefix includes the system rules, extraction schema, and few-shot examples. It repeats across requests, cases, and tenants using the same agent. The source document repeats only for the requests associated with one case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-prompt-bloat-problem"&gt;The prompt bloat problem&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When NavaX launched with its first enterprise tenant, the system prompt was lean. As ArisGlobal onboarded additional pharmaceutical companies, each brought specific requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Custom field mappings (Tenant A maps reaction onset differently from Tenant B).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Regulatory jurisdiction rules (EU ICSR compared with FDA MedWatch and PMDA formatting).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Company-specific business logic for seriousness assessment and causality.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Per-tenant few-shot examples calibrated to each company’s historical cases.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Over 6 months, the shared prompt prefix grew from approximately 72,000 to 180,000 tokens, a 150% increase. With multiple sequential requests per case, per-case inference costs increased by more than 200%.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The prefix grew with every tenant and was reprocessed on every request, so prompt growth compounded with transaction volume.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Table 1 compares the original document-first request order with the production prompt-first design.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Aspect&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;First approach&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Production design&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Request order&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-case document, cache checkpoint, shared prompt prefix&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Shared prompt prefix, cache checkpoint, per-case document&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cached prefix&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The document, which changes for every case&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The shared prompt prefix, which repeats across requests&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reuse scope&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Only the remaining requests for one case&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Requests across cases and tenants using the same agent&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost effect&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The growing prompt is repeatedly processed at the full input rate&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fleet-wide prompt reuse supports sublinear cost growth&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EU routing&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EU geographic cross-Region inference remains unchanged&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EU geographic cross-Region inference remains unchanged&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="amazon-bedrock-prompt-caching-strategies"&gt;Amazon Bedrock prompt caching strategies&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock prompt caching reduces latency and input token costs by caching a contiguous prompt prefix from the start of a request through a cache checkpoint. A later request with the same prefix can read cached tokens at a reduced rate instead of processing them again. Cache entries have a time-to-live (TTL) that resets after a successful cache hit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Caching is prefix-based rather than block-based. Request sections are evaluated in sequence—tools, system, then messages—so changing an earlier section invalidates matching content that follows it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="cache-checkpoints-explicit-control"&gt;Cache checkpoints (explicit control)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With explicit cache checkpoints, you can choose the exact prefix boundary. Supported Claude models allow up to four checkpoints per request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Place each checkpoint after a stable, reusable prefix.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Meet the model-specific minimum prefix size. For Claude Sonnet 4.5 the minimum is 1,024 tokens (see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-supported.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported models and Regions&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Checkpoints are evaluated in this order: tools, system, and messages.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Changing an earlier section invalidates cache matches for later sections.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The default TTL is 5 minutes. Select models support a 1-hour TTL by setting &lt;code&gt;"ttl": "1h"&lt;/code&gt;. Longer-TTL entries must precede shorter-TTL entries.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="simplified-cache-management-automated"&gt;Simplified cache management (automated)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;simplified cache management&lt;/a&gt;, you place one checkpoint after static content. Amazon Bedrock searches backward from that point for the longest matching prefix, up to about 20 content blocks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;One checkpoint can locate the longest matching prefix automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use explicit checkpoints when stable content extends beyond the lookback range.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-arisglobal-cached-and-what-they-deliberately-did-not"&gt;What ArisGlobal cached, and what they deliberately did not&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ArisGlobal uses one explicit checkpoint immediately after the shared prompt prefix, which is sent first. The per-case document follows the checkpoint and is processed on each request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This reverses the initial request order. Because only a prefix can be cached, ordering determines which content can be reused.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-launch-design-prioritizing-document-reuse"&gt;The launch design: Prioritizing document reuse&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first design put the 10–200 page source document at the start of each request and placed the checkpoint after it. The shared prompt prefix and section-specific question followed the checkpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "modelId": "anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929-v1:0",
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "user",
      "content": [
        {
          "text": "[Full adverse event PDF content]..." // &amp;lt;-- Document sent FIRST
        },
        {
          "cachePoint": {"type": "default"} // &amp;lt;-- Checkpoint right after the document
        },
        {
          "text": "[System instructions + compliance rules + schema +
          few-shot examples + customer-specific prompt]..."
          // &amp;lt;-- After the checkpoint: never cached
        },
        {
          "text": "Extract fields for Section 3: Suspect Drug Information"
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The design reused the document within one case: the first request wrote it to cache, and later requests for that case read it at the reduced rate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The next case used a different document and required a new cache write, so reuse never extended across cases or tenants.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;More importantly, the shared prompt prefix remained after the checkpoint and was processed at the full input rate on every request. The content driving the cost curve was excluded from the cache.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-evolved-production-design-prioritizing-shared-prefix-reuse"&gt;The evolved production design: Prioritizing shared-prefix reuse&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The production design changed the order rather than adding another checkpoint: the shared prompt prefix comes first, the checkpoint follows it, and the document comes last.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same checkpoint now creates a reusable fleet-wide cache entry:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Before — the cached document prefix was unique to one case and the shared prompt was never cached.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After — the cached shared prompt prefix repeats across requests, cases, and tenants using the same agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ArisGlobal gave up limited document reuse within one case and gained prompt reuse across the fleet. The shared prefix is also the part that grows as customers are added.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="updated-implementation"&gt;Updated implementation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "modelId": "anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929-v1:0",
  "system": [
    {
      "text": "[Shared system instructions + compliance rules +
      base extraction schema + few-shot examples]..."
    },
    {
      "cachePoint": {"type": "default"} // &amp;lt;-- Checkpoint after the stable prompt
    }
  ],
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "user",
      "content": [
        {
          "text": "[Full adverse event PDF content — sits after the
          checkpoint, not cached]..."
        },
        {
          "text": "Extract fields for Section 5: Event Assessment"
          // &amp;lt;-- Only this changes per request
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For most active accounts, cases arrive about every 5 minutes per tenant and multiple tenants run concurrently. This traffic keeps the shared prefix warm during business hours because each cache hit resets the TTL.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="caching-both-the-prompt-and-the-document"&gt;Caching both the prompt and the document&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A second checkpoint after the document would cache a combined prefix consisting of the shared prompt and that case’s document. The entry would still be unique to one case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ArisGlobal therefore prioritized the prefix whose reads amortize across the fleet. The shared prompt prefix scales across cases and tenants. The per-case document does not.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In production, 65% of input tokens on a typical request are served from the shared prompt cache. Figure 1 shows cache reads consistently exceeding cache writes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 350px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21195-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21195-1.png" alt="Amazon CloudWatch chart with cache reads about 10 times cache writes during production traffic" width="340"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Amazon CloudWatch prompt-cache usage. The blue cache-read series rises to roughly 10 times the cache-write series during periods of production traffic&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Caching the shared prompt prefix produced the following results while the EU geographic cross-Region inference profile remained in place:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;65% of input tokens served from cache at the reduced read rate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Up to 25% lower end-to-end latency on cached production requests.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Approximately 50% lower effective input cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Approximately 40% lower overall monthly inference spend at more than 19 billion tokens per month.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Around 15% more enterprise tenants onboarded without proportional cost increases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;EU geographic routing unchanged. Prompt caching operates within the existing inference configuration.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 516px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21195-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21195-2.png" alt="Line chart showing lower, more stable end-to-end invocation latency after prompt-cache adoption" width="506"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: End-to-end invocation latency. The chart shows a lower and more stable latency range after prompt-cache adoption&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 766px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21195-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21195-3.png" alt="Chart showing sustained invocation count while inference spend grew less than proportionally" width="756"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Invocation count. The chart shows sustained production request volume while inference spend grew less than proportionally&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Based on ArisGlobal’s production billing data, serving 65% of input tokens from cache reduced effective input cost by approximately 50%. At more than 19 billion tokens per month, this produced approximately 40% overall monthly savings in inference spend.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Note: All performance and cost figures in this section reflect ArisGlobal’s production metrics as reported to AWS. Results will vary based on prompt structure, traffic patterns, and cache hit rates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="production-learnings"&gt;Production learnings&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ArisGlobal’s experience offers guidance for many workloads with large, repeated prompt prefixes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cache what repeats, not what is largest.&lt;/strong&gt; A large per-case document offers limited reuse, while a byte-identical shared prompt prefix can serve requests across the fleet.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Put stable content first.&lt;/strong&gt; Order tool definitions, system instructions, schemas, and examples before the checkpoint. Place tenant identifiers, case data, and the per-request question afterward.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use fleet traffic to amortize cache writes.&lt;/strong&gt; Requests from tenants using the same agent can read the same prefix, and cache hits reset the TTL. For sporadic traffic, evaluate the 1-hour TTL on supported models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reduce avoidable calls.&lt;/strong&gt; Each call still processes the uncached document, so bundle sequential questions when output limits and schema requirements permit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep the prefix byte-identical.&lt;/strong&gt; Timestamps, request IDs, tenant names, and reordered tool definitions can cause misses. Move every dynamic element after the checkpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Monitor the production baseline.&lt;/strong&gt; Track cache reads, cache writes, and total input tokens in CloudWatch. Alert on a sustained drop below the normal 65% cache-hit baseline rather than using an incompatible 80% threshold.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Check model-specific limits.&lt;/strong&gt; Prefix minimums and TTL options vary by model. Claude Sonnet 4.5 requires at least 1,024 tokens for a cache checkpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ArisGlobal combined EU geographic cross-Region inference with prompt caching aimed at the right content. Caching the stable shared prefix instead of the per-case document reduced effective input cost by approximately 50%, contributed to approximately 40% overall monthly inference savings, and supported around 15% more enterprise tenants without proportional cost increases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key lesson is that cache value depends on reuse, not size. For multi-tenant agentic workloads, a repeated shared prompt prefix can be more valuable than a larger document that changes for every case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock prompt caching documentation&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cross-region-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cross-Region inference guide&lt;/a&gt;, and the AWS blog post &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/effectively-use-prompt-caching-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Effectively use prompt caching on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
