		<item>
		<title>How Duco transforms financial data automation with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-duco-transforms-financial-data-automation-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rob Gates]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 16 Nov 2026 22:06:08 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">4d56ba5eeb480a2a306a4f4e8ec1bce9830a8eef</guid>

					<description>Duco, a data automation platform for tier-one banks, rebuilt its agentic AI stack on Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. With strict per-tenant isolation, a multi-model strategy, and an MCP skills architecture that cut context overhead by 90%, Duco cut reconciliation deployment time 10x and onboarding costs 70%.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Financial institutions face a critical challenge: processing massive volumes of data with speed, accuracy, and compliance while operating under some of the world’s strictest regulatory frameworks. Duco is the agentic Operations platform for financial services, serving tier-one banks and other financial institutions. It processes 20 billion transactions monthly for 200+ clients, including 7 of the top 20 banks and 10 of the top 20 asset managers. To keep pace as data processing demands grew, Duco evolved from traditional machine learning models to the agentic AI capabilities of Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. In this post, we share how Duco rebuilt its agentic AI stack on these services, cutting deployment time and onboarding costs while keeping each bank’s data isolated.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-scaling-intelligence-in-a-regulated-environment"&gt;The challenge: Scaling intelligence in a regulated environment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco’s platform empowers teams to automate mission-critical data processes using AI and no-code models, handling everything from data extraction to downstream publication. It already used traditional machine learning models for predictive labeling and pattern recognition, helping banks consolidate and automate over 20,000 processes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;However, these classical machine learning approaches had inherent limitations. They required extensive historical data for training, struggled with novel scenarios, and demanded significant customization for each new use case. Most critically, they couldn’t adapt quickly to the diverse, constantly evolving data formats and business rules that characterize modern financial operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“The classical prediction models worked to an extent, but they weren’t agile or flexible enough. Traditional machine learning models can only amplify what they’ve already learned — they can’t help with the next step or find new problems.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Rob Gates, Chief Architect and Innovation Officer, Duco&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-solution-amazon-bedrock-and-bedrock-agentcore"&gt;The solution: Amazon Bedrock and Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco made a strategic decision to adopt &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; for managed access to foundation models, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to run agents at scale. This was more than a technology upgrade. It was a fundamental shift in how its system could understand, reason about, and act on financial data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;About Amazon Bedrock and Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock is a fully managed service that offers a choice of high-performing foundation models from leading AI companies through a single API, along with a broad set of capabilities to build generative AI applications with security, privacy, and responsible AI. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Learn more about Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is the platform to build, connect, and optimize AI agents at scale. Build with any open source framework and any model. Connect agents to your tools and data with authentication and access controls built in. Monitor and improve agents in production with tracing and evaluation. AgentCore handles the infrastructure so you focus on agent logic, not backend plumbing, and you get enterprise security and reliability without giving up open source flexibility.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Learn more on the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; product page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="why-amazon-bedrock"&gt;Why Amazon Bedrock?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Several factors made Amazon Bedrock the ideal choice for Duco’s regulated financial services industry (FSI) environment:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security and compliance first:&lt;/strong&gt; With Amazon Bedrock, customer data stays within the AWS environment, and it is not used to train models. With private endpoints in its virtual private cloud (VPC), Duco can connect to internal systems while keeping them completely isolated, which matters for sensitive financial data processing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model flexibility:&lt;/strong&gt; Access to leading foundation models from the Amazon Nova 2 family, Anthropic, OpenAI, Cohere, Meta, and Mistral AI through a single API means Duco can choose the right model for each task. Duco can switch between models by changing a configuration parameter.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Built for financial services:&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Bedrock provides the robust governance controls and compliance frameworks that financial services require, avoiding infrastructure complexity while maintaining the security standards tier-one banks demand.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="choosing-agentcore-as-the-runtime"&gt;Choosing AgentCore as the runtime&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco’s existing agent infrastructure was built on LangChain and LangGraph, both of which were already in production. When evaluating runtime options for its agentic workloads, AgentCore emerged as the clear choice for three key reasons:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compatibility with existing primitives:&lt;/strong&gt; AgentCore is fully compatible with Duco’s existing LangChain and LangGraph code, so most existing code continued to work without modification.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Managed runtime:&lt;/strong&gt; Instead of Duco running its own agent infrastructure, AgentCore provides a fully managed runtime, reducing operational overhead for the engineering team.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Regulated environment suitability:&lt;/strong&gt; AgentCore met the stringent requirements of deploying in regulated financial services environments, with built-in security and compliance controls.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="multi-tenant-architecture-isolation-by-design"&gt;Multi-tenant architecture: Isolation by design&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco’s platform operates as a multi-tenant software as a service (SaaS) offering with strict tenant isolation (figure 1). This is a non-negotiable requirement for regulated institutions. The architecture reflects this at every layer, from the underlying cloud infrastructure right up to the AI agent runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-20525-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-20525-1.png" alt="Duco multi-tenant architecture with per-tenant isolation across the infrastructure, security, and agent layers" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Multi-tenant architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="infrastructure-layer"&gt;Infrastructure layer&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) clusters&lt;/strong&gt; with namespace-per-tenant isolation and network segregation help maintain workload separation at the Kubernetes layer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Per-tenant dedicated AWS services&lt;/strong&gt;: every tenant receives a dedicated Amazon Aurora MySQL database, Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) storage bucket, AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) encryption keys, Amazon Simple Email Service (Amazon SES) and Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS) messaging stack, Amazon ElastiCache layer, and Amazon Cognito user pool. Zero shared data infrastructure across clients.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Per-tenant autoscaling&lt;/strong&gt; helps minimize the impact of one client’s compute-intensive workload on another tenant’s performance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="security-and-access-control"&gt;Security and access control&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Isolation is enforced through identity as well as infrastructure, with every hop between the user and their data authenticated (figure 2):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tenant-scoped authentication&lt;/strong&gt;: every request carries a JSON Web Token (JWT) issued by the tenant’s dedicated Cognito user pool, validated by the inbound authorizer of the AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, before agent logic runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Gateway-mediated tool access&lt;/strong&gt;: agents reach tools only through the tenant’s dedicated AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, which passes calls through to the tenant’s container-based Model Context Protocol (MCP) services running in Amazon EKS.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;End-to-end user identity&lt;/strong&gt;: the user’s identity is passed through to downstream services, and each MCP server validates both the agent’s and the user’s identity against the tenant’s Cognito user pool, so every tool call is authorized as the calling user, made by a trusted agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Private networking&lt;/strong&gt;: tenants share a VPC and Amazon EKS cluster, segregated by namespace and network policy, with private endpoints keeping traffic to Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore on the AWS network. Each tenant’s AgentCore runtime runs in VPC network mode, with network interfaces in the private subnets, so its outbound traffic enters the VPC and is governed by security groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agent-layer"&gt;Agent layer&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The isolation principle extends fully into the agent architecture:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One AgentCore runtime and Gateway per tenant&lt;/strong&gt;: each tenant is served by a dedicated runtime and Gateway, configured against that tenant’s own Cognito user pool and Amazon EKS namespace. Combined with the built-in session isolation of AgentCore, this means there is no shared memory, context, or tooling between clients. Each bank’s unique schemas, naming conventions, and institutional knowledge remain completely isolated from other tenants.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Per-tenant MCP servers&lt;/strong&gt;: each tenant has its own set of MCP servers providing service-specific capabilities, dynamically discovered at boot time.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture helps maintain a model where each agent only has access to one bank’s data, context, and reasoning, facilitating real isolation of context across banks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-20525-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-20525-2.png" alt="High-level architecture showing requests flowing through a per-tenant AgentCore runtime and Gateway to MCP services in Amazon EKS" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: High-level architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="multi-model-strategy"&gt;Multi-model strategy&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Rather than committing to a single foundation model, Duco uses the right model for each job, assigning each role in its agent design to the model best suited to it. This approach delivers both quality and cost efficiency by matching the right model capability to each specific workload:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Primary role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use cases&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p style="text-align: left"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Anthropic Claude Opus&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Planning agent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;For complex reasoning and understanding, formulating up front plans for other agents to execute on&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Anthropic Claude Sonnet&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Main reasoning &amp;amp; complex decision-making&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Exception investigation, reconciliation logic, rule interpretation&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Anthropic Claude Haiku&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Lightweight tool routing decisions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fast routing choices, low-latency classification, cost-efficient first-pass triage&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Nova 2 Pro&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tool output summarization&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Condensing MCP tool responses, generating readable summaries for analysts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This multi-model approach is architecturally designed to allow model swapping without disrupting the stack, which is critical in a landscape where model capabilities evolve rapidly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solving-context-bloat-the-mcp-skills-architecture"&gt;Solving context bloat: The MCP skills architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One of Duco’s most significant technical innovations addresses a challenge familiar to anyone building production agent systems: context window management.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-problem"&gt;The problem&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With 300–400 tools exposed per tenant through AgentCore Gateway (which fronts a separate MCP server for each underlying Duco service), approximately 200,000 tokens of tool descriptions were loaded into context before work began. This caused &lt;em&gt;context overloading&lt;/em&gt;: response quality degraded as conversations lengthened, and the model lost the thread of earlier reasoning. The team emphasized that a feeling an agent is becoming less capable isn’t enough evidence on its own. Evaluations must be run before engineering decisions are made.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-solution-on-demand-skills-loading"&gt;The solution: On-demand skills loading&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco created a skills architecture using MCP resource types to move tool descriptions out of the static context and load them only when needed:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;skill:// resources&lt;/code&gt; — on-demand tool descriptions loaded only when a skill is required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;system:// resources&lt;/code&gt; — dynamic system prompt sections composed at runtime, keeping the base system prompt lean.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;ui:// resources&lt;/code&gt; — UI-specific resources, isolated from operational tooling.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three new meta-tools, implemented in Duco’s agent harness, replaced the bloated tool description payload:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;load_skill&lt;/code&gt; — fetches skill descriptions on demand, only when the agent determines a skill is needed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;find_tool&lt;/code&gt; — locates relevant tools without loading all schemas into context.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;invoke_tool&lt;/code&gt; — executes the selected tool once identified.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-20525-3.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-20525-3.jpg" alt="On-demand MCP skills loading where meta-tools fetch skill and tool descriptions instead of preloading all tool schemas into context" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Context loading and MCP connectivity&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-results"&gt;The results&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;90% context reduction&lt;/strong&gt;: Main context reduced from 200,000 tokens to approximately 20,000 tokens by moving tool descriptions out of static context and limiting the size of tool responses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prompt caching preserved&lt;/strong&gt;: the static prompt prefix (system prompt plus the three meta-tools) stays small and stable, and skills load into the conversation rather than the tool list, so loading a skill does not invalidate the cache.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Measurably improved agent response quality:&lt;/strong&gt; validated through a rigorous evaluation framework.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Zero infrastructure changes required&lt;/strong&gt;: same underlying models, same tools, and same tasks, with measurably better outcomes.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="mcp-tool-discovery-deploy-a-service-gain-a-capability"&gt;MCP tool discovery: Deploy a service, gain a capability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A key architectural decision was implementing MCP tool discovery at boot time. When a new service is deployed, its MCP server is registered as a target on the tenant’s AgentCore Gateway as part of the deployment. When an agent boots, the harness builds a registry of every tool the Gateway exposes and shares just the tool names. The agent then uses find_tool and invoke_tool to discover the details and invoke tools on demand. As a result, the agent automatically gains that capability with no redeployment or agent reconfiguration required. Access control is enforced by the underlying services themselves: because the user’s identity is passed through the Gateway, each tool call succeeds only if that user is permitted to perform the action.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This provides operational agility: the platform team can ship new capabilities to agents by deploying a new microservice, without touching the agent configuration or requiring downtime. For a platform that serves 200+ clients with diverse and evolving operational needs, this dynamic capability model is a significant competitive advantage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploying-agents-at-scale"&gt;Deploying agents at scale&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco implemented Amazon Bedrock and Bedrock AgentCore to power two critical workflows that previously required extensive manual effort:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="intelligent-onboarding"&gt;1. Intelligent onboarding&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Previously, onboarding new reconciliation processes was a manual, time-intensive exercise requiring significant professional services resources. Banks would take years to migrate thousands of processes, moving at a pace of roughly one per week.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Amazon Bedrock and Bedrock AgentCore, Duco can now:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Automatically understand diverse file formats and data structures across legacy and modern systems.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Translate legacy configurations from existing systems into structured Duco processes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Parse complex spreadsheet macros written decades ago that no one fully understands.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy new reconciliations 10× faster than before.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One global bank experienced a 70% decrease in onboarding costs for new reconciliations and achieved an increase of over 20% in straight-through processing through better match rates and allocation rules.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="ai-driven-exception-investigation"&gt;2. AI-driven exception investigation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Financial institutions run thousands of quality gates between systems, generating countless exceptions daily. Traditionally, armies of analysts manually investigate each break to determine if it’s worth worrying about, already being investigated, or represents a new risk.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco’s agents now handle the “first mile” of investigation:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Triage&lt;/strong&gt; new breaks as they arrive, grouping related exceptions that share a likely root cause.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Check context&lt;/strong&gt;, such as whether a break is already known, already being worked, or a timing difference expected to clear.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Investigate&lt;/strong&gt; by pulling evidence from source data and systems to trace the root cause of each inconsistency, whether a data quality issue, a missing or late record, a mapping or reference data problem, or a genuine mismatch, and explain why it happened.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommend&lt;/strong&gt; a decision-ready case: the likely cause, a proposed resolution such as an adjustment, a rule change, or an escalation, the alternatives considered, and the supporting evidence.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Route&lt;/strong&gt; each case to the right analyst, who reviews the evidence and makes the final call, with every decision recorded in the audit trail.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This agentic approach excels because financial workflows involve highly variable paths through information, exactly the kind of complex, context-heavy reasoning that foundation models handle well.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="operating-in-a-regulated-environment"&gt;Operating in a regulated environment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploying AI agents in financial services requires more than technical capability. It demands rigorous compliance, auditability, and risk management. Duco’s implementation on Amazon Bedrock addresses these requirements through four core principles:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Comprehensive observability:&lt;/strong&gt; Every agent decision is logged in a complete audit trail. Reviewers can trace each decision to check it against regulatory requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human-in-the-loop controls:&lt;/strong&gt; High-risk actions require explicit authorization, with clear escalation pathways for complex scenarios that exceed agent confidence thresholds.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fail-safe architecture:&lt;/strong&gt; Systems default to client protection over operational efficiency, supporting safety even when agents encounter edge cases or unexpected data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Regulatory alignment:&lt;/strong&gt; The platform supports workloads subject to SOC 2 and DORA requirements, with data residency controls per institution.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results-that-matter"&gt;Results that matter&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco’s adoption of Amazon Bedrock has delivered measurable, auditable business impact across client deployments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p style="text-align: center"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;10×&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p style="text-align: center"&gt;Faster reconciliation deployments&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="text-align: center"&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;70%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Reduction in onboarding costs&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="text-align: center"&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;20%+&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Increase in straight-through processing&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p style="text-align: center"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1000s&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p style="text-align: center"&gt;Hours saved in manual investigation&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Perhaps most importantly, Duco now has a foundation for rapid innovation. Building new agentic capabilities no longer requires extensive machine learning engineering, so the team can focus on solving business problems rather than managing infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="roadmap-the-future-of-humanagent-collaboration"&gt;Roadmap: The future of human–agent collaboration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duco’s architecture is designed not to replace workers, but to elevate them. The platform envisions a clear progression in human roles as agentic capabilities mature:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Task Worker&lt;/strong&gt; →&amp;nbsp;&lt;strong&gt;Investigator&lt;/strong&gt; →&amp;nbsp;&lt;strong&gt;Decision Maker&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Rather than humans performing repetitive tasks, the future sees operations teams orchestrating multiple agents in parallel with humans making final approval decisions based on agent-prepared analysis. The architecture is designed to support agent-to-agent communication, including cross-firm scenarios where agents at different financial institutions could coordinate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For regulated financial institutions, Duco’s architecture shows that agentic AI isn’t only viable. It’s already delivering at production scale. Duco rebuilt its agentic AI stack on Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, combining strict per-tenant isolation, the right model for each job, and an MCP skills architecture that cut context overhead by 90%. The results include reconciliation deployments 10× faster and, for one global bank, onboarding costs down 70% and straight-through processing up by over 20%. To build a similar multi-tenant setup, start with the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;, then read &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/building-multi-tenant-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Building multi-tenant agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; on the AWS Machine Learning Blog. For the operating model behind this work, see Duco’s &lt;a href="https://du.co/agentic-operations-new-operating-model-for-post-trade/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agentic Operations: a new operating model for post-trade&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Taxonomy of multi-agent design patterns, anti-patterns, and lessons from manufacturing</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/taxonomy-of-multi-agent-design-patterns-anti-patterns-and-lessons-from-manufacturing/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Malini Sethi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 16 Nov 2026 21:53:46 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">6fae81f2f51c263e67f03ac10a33149b6fedcdd0</guid>

					<description>Multi-agent coordination is a distributed systems problem, not a model problem. This post gives a field-tested taxonomy of multi-agent design patterns and anti-patterns, a framework for deciding when multiple agents are warranted, and how to build cost-efficient, reliable systems on AWS.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Enterprise teams are building multi-agent systems, and when early implementations fall short, the cause is rarely the model. Multi-agent coordination is a distributed systems problem, and many teams taking it on haven’t yet built distributed systems instincts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The numbers are consistent across sources: &lt;a href="https://www.cio.com/article/3850763/88-of-ai-pilots-fail-to-reach-production-but-thats-not-all-on-it.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;~88% of AI pilots never reach production&lt;/a&gt;. Stanford HAI demonstrated that &lt;a href="https://hai.stanford.edu/news/ai-coding-agents-fail-at-teamwork" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;two models can perform worse collaborating&lt;/a&gt; than either alone when coordination overhead exceeds the benefit. The pattern is clear: teams are adding complexity faster than they build understanding. Ten steps that are each 95 percent&amp;nbsp;reliable deliver only 59.9 percent end-to-end reliability, and 20 steps deliver 35.8 percent. This illustrative model assumes a strictly sequential chain with no retries, but the direction holds: each dependent handoff multiplies risk.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learn how to decide whether a use case needs multiple agents, choose a coordination pattern, and spot costly anti-patterns early. You also see how takt time, safety boundaries, edge data, and shift cycles shape the architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="multi-agent-coordination-patterns"&gt;Multi-agent coordination patterns&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Coordination patterns fall into two tiers, based on who controls the flow. In Tier 1, you define the path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In Tier 2, the model chooses the path at runtime. That flexibility handles work whose steps you can’t define in advance, at a higher cost in tokens, latency, and debugging. The practical default is to start with Tier 1 and move a task to Tier 2 only when its subtasks emerge during the work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tier-1-patterns-deterministic-control-flow"&gt;Tier 1: Patterns (deterministic control flow)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1.1 Sequential Pipeline:&lt;/strong&gt; Orchestration patterns that implement linear execution where each node’s output feeds into the next node (A -&amp;gt; B -&amp;gt; C) supporting ordered processing with predictable execution timing&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the following diagram, input flows to agent A (extract), then agent B (transform), then agent C (validate), and then output. The user defines that order at build time, and nothing is decided at runtime. As a result, the pipeline is straightforward to debug and test, and its cost is predictable. Typical fits include document processing, extract, transform, and load (ETL) chains, and warranty-claim triage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-1-1.png" alt="Sequential pipeline: input flows through extract, transform, and validate agents to output" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Sequential pipeline where each agent’s output feeds the next, from extract to transform to validate&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1.2 Conditional Workflow:&lt;/strong&gt; Logic-based edges select the next node by condition, so the workflow can branch and send each case down a different path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the following diagram, a user request goes to agent A (write code) and then to agent B (test code). A pass/fail check sends passing work to agent D (deploy). Failing work loops back to agent A for a retry, up to a maximum of N times, and then escalates to a human. The orchestration layer enforces that cap, for example through an AWS Step Functions iteration limit, which keeps the loop from becoming the unbounded iteration anti-pattern described later in this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-2-1.png" alt="Conditional workflow where passing work from the test agent deploys and failing work loops back to the write agent" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Conditional workflow that retries failures up to N times before escalating to a human&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1.3 Conditional Router&lt;/strong&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A classification step examines the input and directs it to the appropriate processing path. Only one path is executed per request. Best for intent-driven dispatch, quality-gate decisions, customer inquiry routing, and anomaly triage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows a plant supervisor’s request being read by a classifier and sent to exactly one specialist. Questions about whether parts meet spec go to the Quality Agent, overdue equipment services go to the Maintenance Agent, and shift changes go to the Scheduling Agent. This decision is made at the entry point.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-3-1.png" alt="Conditional router sending each request down a single classified processing path" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Conditional router that runs one path per request, with bounded retries before human escalation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1.4 Parallel Workflow:&lt;/strong&gt; A task splits into independent subtasks that run simultaneously across handlers, each on its own data. A fan-in step combines the results when all handlers complete, and a failure in one path retries only that path. Best for multi-source analysis, batch processing, and work that decomposes into independent pieces.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the following diagram, a research orchestrator decomposes the task and dispatches it to three specialist agents that run concurrently: a financial data agent, a geopolitical agent, and a logistics agent, each analyzing its own source. A combined step waits for all three, then synthesizes the results, resolves conflicts, and ranks the findings. Latency equals the slowest single agent rather than the sum of all three.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-4-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-4-1.png" alt="Parallel workflow fanning out to financial, geopolitical, and logistics agents that run concurrently" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Parallel fan-out to specialist agents followed by a fan-in step that synthesizes and ranks the results&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tier-2-autonomous-patterns-llm-determined-control-flow"&gt;Tier 2: Autonomous patterns (LLM-determined control flow)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In autonomous patterns, control flow is determined at runtime by the AI model itself, not predefined by developers. Agents read task state, reason about what’s needed and coordinate through shared context rather than fixed paths spanning a spectrum from structured orchestration to fully emergent behavior.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="plan-based-orchestration"&gt;2.1 Plan-based orchestration&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In plan-based orchestration, a single orchestrator agent acts as a project manager. The orchestrator maintains an explicit task plan with assignments and dependencies, sees all context while each worker receives only its slice, distributes work dynamically against the current plan, and monitors progress centrally. Key design choice is that the workers are stateless and independent. If one fails, it doesn’t contaminate others. The orchestrator retries only the failed worker, not the entire workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how a planner agent decomposes a task into an explicit plan and dispatches each step to a worker’s agent. Each step goes to a dedicated worker (a research agent, an analysis agent, and a report agent) that sees only its assigned slice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-5-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-5-1.png" alt="Plan-based orchestration where a planner agent dispatches steps to research, analysis, and report workers" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Plan-based orchestration with a planner agent assigning each step to a stateless worker agent&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="handoff-pattern"&gt;2.2 Handoff pattern&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents operate with limited, local knowledge and coordinate through peer-to-peer delegation. Each makes local decisions about when and to whom to transfer control, based on the task and the agents it knows. Control passes with state: each transfer carries validated task state, the evidence behind it, and any unresolved questions, so the receiving agent continues rather than reinterpreting a summary. A handoff that moves only through the conversation is not a handoff.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the following diagram, a customer service agent transfers control, together with its context, to either a technical support agent or a billing agent, and technical support can hand off further to a senior engineer. Each agent knows only the small set of peers it can transfer to.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-6-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-6-1.png" alt="Handoff pattern where a customer service agent transfers control to a technical support or billing agent" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Handoff pattern passing validated state between peer agents through local delegation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="conversation-driven-pattern"&gt;2.3 Conversation-driven pattern&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In conversation-driven patterns (also known as group chat patterns), all agents participate in a shared conversation where orchestration emerges through turn-taking rather than explicit plans or structured handoffs. This mirrors human team collaboration where everyone can see the discussion and contribute when appropriate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the following diagram, an agent-selection mechanism decides whose turn it is, and agents A, B, and C each add to one shared conversation, where every turn is a message plus a tool call. The selection mechanism sets the turn-taking, and state lives implicitly in the conversation history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-7-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-7-1.png" alt="Conversation-driven pattern where a selection mechanism gives turns to agents A, B, and C in a shared conversation" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Conversation-driven pattern where turn-taking selects the next agent and state lives in the conversation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Control flow characteristics:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Visibility: Broadcast all agents observe all messages in the shared conversation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Decision making: Next speaker selected based on conversation context, not predetermined plans.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;State management: Implicit in the conversation history.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn-taking: Determined by selection mechanisms (round-robin, AI-driven) 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Round-robin. Agents take turns in a fixed, repeating order until a termination condition is met, which keeps it predictable.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;AI-driven. A model selects the next speaker from the conversation context rather than a fixed order.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="debateconsensus"&gt;2.4 Debate/consensus&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Multiple agents independently evaluate the same input, and their conclusions are reconciled by majority, by a judge agent, or by escalation when they disagree. Unlike a conversation-driven pattern, the agents do not see each other’s reasoning before committing to a position, which is what makes disagreement informative rather than socially convergent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use it sparingly. Debate is the most expensive pattern here, and on perceptual tasks it buys latency rather than accuracy (see the quality-inspection case in anti-pattern 3 that follows). Reserve it for high-stakes, low-volume decisions where being wrong costs more than deliberating.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="hybrid-approaches"&gt;Hybrid approaches&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In practice, most production systems combine several patterns based on task decomposition:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use&lt;em&gt; workflow patterns&lt;/em&gt; for predictable, well-understood components.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Apply &lt;em&gt;autonomous patterns &lt;/em&gt;where flexibility and adaptation are essential.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Implement &lt;em&gt;task management patterns&lt;/em&gt; (termination, delegation) consistently across all orchestration types.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Design &lt;em&gt;hierarchical compositions&lt;/em&gt; where complex agents internally use different patterns at different layers.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The governing principle: &lt;em&gt;pattern-to-task alignment&lt;/em&gt;. Match the orchestration approach to each task’s inherent characteristics and your system’s reliability requirements rather than forcing everything through a single pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="anti-patterns-that-burn-budget"&gt;Anti-patterns that burn budget&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each of the following five anti-patterns looks reasonable at design time, and each surfaces later as an inflated token cost, a missed takt-time window, or a decision that has quietly drifted out of sync with the plant floor.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="god-agent"&gt;1. God agent&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One agent owns overall equipment effectiveness (OEE) analysis, programmable logic controller (PLC) diagnostics, and maintenance behind a single prompt with 15 or more tools. Tool selection degrades as tools grow, and failures become hard to attribute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The fix:&lt;/strong&gt; Bounded agents of five tools or fewer, such as an OEE analyst, a fault diagnostician, and a maintenance drafter.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="unbounded-iteration"&gt;2. Unbounded iteration&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent loops on “try again” with no hard stop. A four-agent system can burn a significantly higher number of tokens before a single retry, and no one call trips an alarm. Retries multiply coordination failures rather than fixing them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The fix:&lt;/strong&gt; Hard iteration ceiling in code, per-agent token ceiling, budget alarm wired to an automatic stop. Infrastructure enforces limits. The model will not stop itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="natural-language-contracts"&gt;3. Natural-language contracts&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents hand each other free-text prose with no schema. Interpretation hardens at every hop: “could indicate wear” becomes “confirmed wear” becomes a maintenance order. The drift happens between agents, where tracing is thinnest.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="premature-multi-agent"&gt;4. Premature multi-agent&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Multi-agent where one agent with tool groups would do. Each agent adds a coordination tax that is not linear, and the gains flatten around four. Most “specialists” are one model in different costumes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The fix:&lt;/strong&gt; Needs at least two different models, different tool access, concurrent execution, and real interdependency. Fewer than two, and one agent with action groups does it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;The test: can one agent handle the phases by switching tools? Then it is not a multi-agent problem.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="unguarded-customer-contact"&gt;5. Unguarded customer contact&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent triggers real-world actions (email, service visits, machine parameters) with no approval step. Hallucinations cross into the physical world, irreversible actions compound, and liability escalates from a refund to product liability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The fix:&lt;/strong&gt; Defense in depth: recommendation → Amazon Bedrock Guardrails → schema validation → business rules → human approval → execution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every point where agent output reaches a customer, or a safety system is a trust boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choosing-an-architecture"&gt;Choosing an architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the patterns and anti-patterns in view, the architecture choice comes down to a short sequence of questions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Does the task require action beyond text generation?&lt;/strong&gt; Treat a direct model call as the baseline and add orchestration only for a measured reason. Summarizing documents or generating code from a specification rarely supplies one.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Is the solution path known in advance?&lt;/strong&gt; If you can write down what happens in what order, you don’t have a “how many agents” decision at all. Your real choice is simpler: a single agent that calls tools, or a Tier 1 workflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Does the work require genuinely distinct expertise?&lt;/strong&gt; Separate domains with established handoffs justify specialized agents. A single domain belongs to one agent, no matter how many tools it needs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Does the solution emerge only through exploration?&lt;/strong&gt; When the action sequence is known and can run as an act-and-validate loop, it belongs to a single agent, even one that plans its steps dynamically. Tier 2 earns its added cost, latency, and debugging burden only when the subtasks themselves are discovered in the course of the work.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following decision tree turns these four questions into a path you can follow from top to bottom.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-8-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-8-1.png" alt="Decision tree for choosing an architecture based on whether a task needs actions, a known path, distinct expertise, or exploration" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: Decision tree for choosing the simplest architecture, from a single model call to a Tier 2 multi-agent pattern&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The tree starts by asking whether the task requires actions beyond text generation. If it doesn’t, a simple model call is enough. If it does, the tree asks whether the solution is well known and can be expressed as a workflow. On the known solution side, work that benefits from specialized expertise or domain separation go to a Tier 1 multi-agent workflow (a pipeline, fan-out, or router). Everything else goes to a single agent with tools and action groups. On the unknown-solution side, only tasks that involve exploration or significant side effects go to Tier 2 for autonomous multi-agent patterns. The rest stays with a single agent. The guiding principle is to choose the simplest architecture that meets your requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following chart compares the patterns on four things: when to reach for each one, how control flows between agents, how deterministic the result is, and what it costs to build and operate. Build cost is a relative scale, not an estimate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-9-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21577-9-1.png" alt="Chart comparing the patterns by when to use them, control flow, determinism, and build cost" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: Comparison of the patterns by use case, control flow, determinism, and relative build cost&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tier 1 patterns score high on determinism at low to moderate build cost. Tier 2 patterns give more of the path to the model, and plan-based orchestration costs the most to build. If you can draw the control flow on a whiteboard before runtime, start with Tier 1.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="applied-mapping-patterns-to-manufacturing-use-cases"&gt;Applied: Mapping patterns to manufacturing use cases&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use Case&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pattern&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Rationale&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Shift handoff summarization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sequential Pipeline&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Linear: raw logs → summary → report&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-station root-cause analysis&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Plan-Based Orchestrator&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Dynamic: subtasks discovered during analysis&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Real-time quality gate&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Conditional Router + Edge Agent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;In-cycle: local model decides. Agent reasoning off path&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Predictive maintenance scheduling&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Parallel Fan-Out/Fan-In&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Independent models per asset class&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Engineering change order impact&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Debate/Consensus&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;High stakes: adversarial review justified&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Supplier communication drafting&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sequential + Human Gate&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Must not auto-send (see Unguarded Customer Contact)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cross-plant optimization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Plan-Based Orchestrator&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multi-source: dynamic queries. Solver does allocation&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-manufacturing-lens-four-constraints-that-change-the-calculus"&gt;The manufacturing lens: Four constraints that change the calculus&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Manufacturing is not a generic enterprise IT environment, and four constraints make that arithmetic unforgiving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="constraint-1-production-line-latency-direct-revenue-loss"&gt;Constraint 1: Production-line latency = direct revenue loss&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Automotive body shops, where robots weld the vehicle body together, typically run at 60 jobs per hour. Each of their over 40 stations has a 60-second takt time before the line advances. Inference latency that sits on a station’s cycle path eats into those 60 seconds, and a delay at the bottleneck station slows the whole line. Even a 2 percent throughput reduction can translate to significant annual revenue impact.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Architecture implication:&lt;/strong&gt; budget latency at p99, not average. The tail is what stops the line. Give every agent a sub-second deterministic fallback, and match it to the risk:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Advisory/quality-flagging:&lt;/strong&gt; Pass the part with a flag for end-of-line review.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Safety-critical:&lt;/strong&gt; Containment or a deterministic stop, not continuation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Decide this mapping per station before deployment.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="constraint-2-safety-has-physical-consequences"&gt;Constraint 2: Safety has physical consequences&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A hallucinated chatbot causes embarrassment. A hallucinated welding parameter causes structural defects that surface in crash testing or field failure and can lead to a safety recall. Two failure modes recur:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;OT/IT boundary state desync:&lt;/strong&gt; An agent reads the manufacturing execution system (MES) as Station 12 running Part #A, but a manual PLC override already switched it to Part #B, so it tunes parameters for the wrong part.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hallucinated safety boundary:&lt;/strong&gt; A maintenance agent reasons about lockout/tagout from scratch and declares Station 7 accessible while adjacent Station 6 runs, breaking the interlock zone.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Architecture implication:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The boundary: keep the deterministic core unchanged, including PLCs, supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) systems, and the MES, and wrap an advisory layer around it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agents observe, analyze, and recommend. They do not actuate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Safety constraints live as deterministic guardrails in an external service that agents query rather than reason about. They’re never embedded in prompts, where they can be argued away.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validation and approval:&lt;/strong&gt; A recommendation passes schema validation and physics simulation, checked against the OT source of truth immediately before actuation, never against OT state cached beyond one cycle. It’s then approved at a level matched to its scope:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Level 0:&lt;/strong&gt; Agent resolves autonomously.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Level 1:&lt;/strong&gt; Operator confirms with one click.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Level 2:&lt;/strong&gt; Engineer decides from the presented analysis.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Level 3:&lt;/strong&gt; Management evaluates a line-stop recommendation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Only then does a validated command interface pass permitted commands to deterministic control. The large language model (LLM) proposes. The physics engine and the human operator dispose.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="constraint-3-data-lives-at-the-edge"&gt;Constraint 3: Data lives at the edge&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A single stamping press generates 50+ GB/day of sensor data whose value decays in milliseconds. Shipping it to the cloud costs more in bandwidth than compute, and by the time the cloud answers, the press has moved on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Architecture implication:&lt;/strong&gt; hybrid edge-cloud is the practical default, and the Constraint 2 boundary holds at the edge too.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Edge (AWS IoT Greengrass):&lt;/strong&gt; sub-takt anomaly detection, threshold monitoring, and stop-or-continue signals at the source: the edge agent recommends, the PLC acts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cloud:&lt;/strong&gt; What the edge can’t do: cross-shift analytics, trend correlation across plants, and model retraining on fleet data. The edge distills and transmits only what matters.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="constraint-4-shift-aligned-agent-lifecycle"&gt;Constraint 4: Shift-aligned agent lifecycle&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Unbounded context makes an agent drift as stale observations crowd out current reality. A full reset loses continuity between shifts. Manufacturing already solves this for humans with structured shift handoffs, and agents need the same discipline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Architecture implication:&lt;/strong&gt; Bound agent memory to the shift cycle.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Initialize:&lt;/strong&gt; Shift context, including the crew roster, production targets, and the prior shift handoff.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Accumulate:&lt;/strong&gt; Working memory within the shift window only.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hand off:&lt;/strong&gt; Emit a shift-end summary of open issues, flagged anomalies, and recommendations that seed the next crew, mirroring the human ritual without unbounded context growth.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="building-on-aws"&gt;Building on AWS&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following managed services map to the concerns raised earlier in this post. Each row names the mechanism, not just the service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Need&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Service&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mechanism&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Runtime&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Where agents run, their control flow is determined or model determined. Bring Strands, LangGraph, or CrewAI.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Shift-bounded memory&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/memory.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore memory&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Managed short-term and long-term context, so the shift-scoped memory from Constraint 4 is configuration rather than custom state plumbing.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Typed handoffs&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Converts APIs and AWS Lambda functions into Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools with validated schemas, so handoffs carry typed fields rather than prose.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent permissions&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A unique, verifiable identity for each agent with fine-grained access control. It’s the enforcement point for which agent can reach which system, and on whose behalf.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tracing between agents&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Spans and traces across runtime, memory, gateway, and tools, through Amazon CloudWatch Transaction Search and AWS Distro for Open Telemetry (ADOT). Handoffs are the least observable part of a multi-agent system and where untyped contracts fail silently.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human approval surface&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Where the escalation tiers land. AWS Quick Automate runs approval workflows with one-click approval in Slack, and its action connectors can invoke Amazon Bedrock AgentCore agents.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tiered model selection&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One API across model families, so cost tiering is configuration: a small model for a router’s classification and a stronger one for an orchestrator synthesis.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion-from-patterns-to-production"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Multi-agent systems change how automotive manufacturers orchestrate intelligence at scale, but only when built on the right foundations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The patterns and anti-patterns here come from real deployments where poor architecture shows up as runaway token costs, takt-time violations, and, at worst, physical safety risks. The economics at plant scale are unforgiving, because token volume grows with every agent, robot, and shift. Tiered model selection, context compression, and edge-cloud distribution are the main levers for delivering the same value at a fraction of that cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The recommendation here is to start with Tier 1 patterns, prove value, and then expand. Most manufacturing use cases, such as shift handoff summarization, quality-gate decisions, and predictive maintenance, are well served by deterministic workflows. Reserve autonomous orchestration for genuinely open-ended problems where dynamic subtask discovery justifies the added cost, latency, and debugging complexity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
